	<item>
		<title>Full Fine-Tuning (FFT) on SageMaker AI Serverless Model Customization</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/full-fine-tuning-fft-on-sagemaker-ai-serverless-model-customization/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Sandeep Raveesh-Babu]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 19 Nov 2026 00:15:58 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">bc37888f62d641a4c4d60233598cdb1fdb44d420</guid>

					<description>Amazon SageMaker AI Serverless Model Customization now supports Full Fine-Tuning (FFT). This post explores when FFT outperforms LoRA across key benchmarks, examines the spectral properties of model weight matrices behind the quality gap, shares practical hyperparameter tuning guidelines, and shows how to get started.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Fine-tuning is the process of continuing to train a pretrained large language model (LLM) on a company’s own data so it adapts to a specific domain, task, or style rather than relying on general-purpose knowledge alone. For businesses, the value is twofold. A fine-tuned model delivers measurably better accuracy on domain-specific work while reducing costs: a smaller specialized model can match or exceed a frontier model on your task at a fraction of the per-token inference price. Those savings compound across millions of requests. Fine-tuning has therefore become an essential process for businesses looking to get more value from LLMs at lower costs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods like Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) have emerged as a practical solution for fine-tuning LLMs to downstream tasks, reducing GPU capacity requirements by updating only a small subset of LLM parameters. LoRA aims to strike a balance between efficiency and expressiveness, however, the low-rank nature of the weight update inherently limits the representation power of LoRA fine-tuned models, potentially compromising performance on complex tasks. This trade-off hampers the ability of LoRA methods to capture complex patterns needed for diverse tasks. To address these challenges, Full Fine-Tuning (FFT) updates model parameters simultaneously, overcoming the low-rank limitations so LLMs can fully absorb complex domain-specific knowledge.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/model-customization/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI Serverless Model Customization&lt;/a&gt; now supports &lt;a href="http://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/customizing-models-fft.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Full Fine-Tuning (FFT)&lt;/a&gt;, so you can achieve superior model quality without managing infrastructure. There is no GPU provisioning, cluster setup, or distributed-training configuration required. In this post, we explore when and where FFT consistently outperforms LoRA across key benchmarks, examine the spectral properties of model weight matrices that explain this quality gap, provide practical hyperparameter tuning guidelines backed by controlled experiments using SageMaker AI Serverless Model Customization, and show you how to get started with FFT on SageMaker AI.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="full-fine-tuning-why-it-matters"&gt;Full Fine-Tuning: Why it matters&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Model customization types like LoRA reduce trainable parameters by freezing the pre-trained model’s weights and injecting small, separate pairs of replacement matrices (called low-rank matrices) into the model layers. This works well for simple adaptations, but the low-rank structure inherently limits the representation power of the fine-tuned model. As tasks grow more complex and datasets scale beyond tens of thousands of examples, LoRA hits an accuracy plateau where increasing rank (and thus trainable parameters) fails to bridge the accuracy gap with standard fine-tuning. This plateau reveals that the performance gap isn’t simply because of fewer parameters. It’s caused by rank deficiency itself. The low-rank update can’t capture the full complexity of the weight changes needed for deep task adaptation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;However, for complex tasks and domains where large, good-quality datasets exist, full-rank fine-tuning excels by updating model parameters simultaneously. FFT modifies the model’s weight matrix uniformly, using the full representational capacity of the model’s weight matrices rather than restricting updates to a small subspace, and without introducing the abrupt, localized distortions characteristic of low-rank methods. The result is stronger model convergence, better activation alignment across model layers, and a model that faithfully captures both the target task distribution and the richness of its pre-trained knowledge. FFT’s quality advantage grows with dataset scale: at 100K+ training examples, FFT achieved 90.0 percent on LLM-as-Judge semantic correctness across code generation tasks, as shown in the following chart.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-1.png" alt="LLM-as-Judge semantic correctness by training method as training data scales to 100K-plus examples" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: LLM-as-Judge semantic correctness by training method as training data scales to 100K+ examples&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Furthermore, the following radar chart provides a holistic view: the base model (inner polygon) shows consistently low scores across all axes. LoRA (middle polygon) shows strong, balanced improvement. FFT (outer polygon) extends further on every dimension, with particularly pronounced leads on BLEU and ROUGE-2 scores. This multi-dimensional quality lead confirms that it isn’t a metric-specific artifact but a fundamental advantage of full-rank optimization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-2.png" alt="Radar chart comparing the base model, LoRA, and FFT across BLEU, ROUGE, and correctness metrics" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Radar chart comparing the base model, LoRA, and FFT across quality dimensions&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The details of how we arrived at these results, the coding datasets used for model customization, and the experimentation process are explained in detail in the following sections.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="experimentation-and-benchmark-results"&gt;Experimentation and benchmark results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post we used the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/customizing-models-sft.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) technique&lt;/a&gt; on a Qwen3-4B base model offered within &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI Studio&lt;/a&gt; and performed all the fine-tuning experiments using &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/customize-model.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker AI Serverless Model Customization&lt;/a&gt; across LoRA and FFT training methods. Within each experiment, both methods received the same prompts, targets, and evaluation code.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="dataset-code-generation-110k-instruction-pairs"&gt;Dataset: Code generation 110K instruction pairs&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For all the experimentation, we fine-tuned the Qwen3-4B base model on the &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/datasets/ise-uiuc/Magicoder-Evol-Instruct-110K" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Magicoder-Evol-Instruct-110K&lt;/a&gt; dataset, a curated corpus of 110,000 coding instruction-response pairs spanning algorithm design, debugging, data structures, and systems programming. We applied a 90/5/5 split to the dataset: 99,000 records for training, 5,500 for validation to monitor convergence and training loss during the customization job, and 5,500 reserved as a held-out test set for unbiased evaluation of the final model, as registered in SageMaker AI dataset assets and shown in the following figure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-3.png" alt="The 110K instruction-pair dataset with train, validation, and test splits registered in SageMaker AI dataset assets" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: The 110K instruction-pair dataset registered in SageMaker AI dataset assets&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="serverless-model-customization-job"&gt;Serverless Model Customization job&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;All the model customization experiments in this post were performed on Amazon SageMaker AI Serverless Model Customization. Serverless Model Customization on SageMaker AI lets data scientists and machine learning (ML) engineers focus on model fine-tuning without managing infrastructure or GPU capacity planning. You specify the base model, dataset, and hyperparameters, and the service handles compute allocation and checkpoint management automatically in a serverless fashion. The following code snippet shows one such experiment for launching a Full Fine-Tuning (FFT) job on Serverless Model Customization using the SageMaker Python SDK.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from sagemaker.train.sft_trainer import SFTTrainer
from sagemaker.train.common import TrainingType

trainer = SFTTrainer(
    model=base_model_id,
    training_type=TrainingType.FULL,
    model_package_group=model_package_group_name,
    training_dataset=training_dataset,
    validation_dataset=val_dataset,
    s3_output_path=output_path,
    sagemaker_session=sess,
    role=role,
)

# Configure hyperparameters as needed
trainer.hyperparameters.learning_rate = 2e-5
trainer.hyperparameters.global_batch_size = 8
trainer.hyperparameters.max_epochs = 2
trainer.hyperparameters.lr_warmup_steps_ratio = 0.1

trainer.train(wait=False)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The code triggers a serverless model customization job, shown in the following screenshot, with training performance metrics tracked in the job and viewable in the linked MLflow app.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-4.png" alt="Serverless model customization job in SageMaker AI with training metrics tracked and a linked MLflow app" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Serverless model customization job with training metrics tracked in MLflow&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Switching from Full Fine-Tuning to LoRA requires only one change: set &lt;code&gt;TrainingType.FULL&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;TrainingType.LORA&lt;/code&gt;. The rest of the code remains identical:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;trainer = SFTTrainer(
    model=base_model_id,
    training_type=TrainingType.LORA,  # ← only change
    model_package_group=model_package_group_name,
    training_dataset=training_dataset,
    validation_dataset=val_dataset,
    ...
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="model-evaluation-and-benchmarking"&gt;Model evaluation and benchmarking&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We tested the resulting customized models across three diverse &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/model-customize-evaluation-types.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;evaluation methods&lt;/a&gt; built into SageMaker AI Serverless Model Customization: LLM-as-Judge, PRIMECODE, and MMLU, shown in the following image. Each targets a different dimension of model quality: semantic correctness, code generation fidelity, and general knowledge retention. Together they provide a comprehensive picture of the model customization quality.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-5.png" alt="The three built-in evaluation methods (LLM-as-Judge, PRIMECODE, and MMLU) in SageMaker AI Serverless Model Customization" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: The three built-in evaluation methods in SageMaker AI Serverless Model Customization&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The LLM-as-Judge metric uses a frontier model (in this post, Amazon Nova Pro) as an automated evaluator that reads the model’s generated code alongside the original prompt and judges whether the solution is logically correct. On serverless model customization you can evaluate both the customized model alongside the base model, as shown in the following figure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-6.png" alt="LLM-as-Judge evaluation comparing the customized model and the base model in SageMaker AI" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: LLM-as-Judge evaluation comparing the customized model and the base model&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;PRIMECODE is a custom scorer evaluation that measures how closely the model’s generated code matches reference solutions using deterministic text-similarity metrics: BLEU (n-gram precision), ROUGE-1/2/L (recall-oriented overlap), and F1 (precision-recall balance). These metrics are fully reproducible, making them ideal for controlled ablation studies. We used PRIMECODE as our primary metric for surface-level code quality because it quantifies how well the model has learned the coding patterns, formatting conventions, and implementation style present in the training data. A high BLEU score means the model generates code that looks structurally similar to expert-written solutions, as shown in the following results.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-7.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-7.png" alt="PRIMECODE text-similarity scores (BLEU, ROUGE, and F1) for the customized model" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: PRIMECODE text-similarity scores for the customized model&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;MMLU (Massive Multitask Language Understanding) is a built-in benchmark that tests the model across 57 academic subjects from abstract algebra and anatomy to Nutrition and Philosophy. It measures whether the model retains its broad general knowledge after domain-specific fine-tuning, as shown in the following figure. We used MMLU as our forgetting detection guardrail. Fine-tuning on code can overwrite general capabilities stored in the model’s weights. By tracking MMLU accuracy before and after fine-tuning, we can quantify exactly how much general knowledge the model sacrifices for code generation improvement, and whether our hyperparameter choices accelerate or mitigate this trade-off.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-8.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-8.png" alt="MMLU accuracy across 57 subjects before and after domain-specific fine-tuning" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 8: MMLU accuracy before and after fine-tuning&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="rapid-experimentation-at-scale"&gt;Rapid experimentation at scale&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The ease of setup and use of SageMaker AI Serverless Model Customization let us perform rapid experimentation without re-provisioning infrastructure. We changed training types between LoRA and FFT, swept hyperparameters, and tested across multiple base models. Over a short time window, we ran tens of model customization experiments across &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/model-customize-open-weight.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;multiple supported base models&lt;/a&gt; (Qwen3-4B, DeepSeek-R1-1.5B, Nemotron-30B, Gemma-4-26B), systematically ablating learning rate, batch size, warmup ratio, epoch count, and dataset scale. Each experiment, from job submission to evaluation results, completed in a few hours and the resulting fine-tuned models were automatically registered in SageMaker Model Registry as versioned Model Packages, ready for review or deployment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="hyperparameter-options"&gt;Hyperparameter options&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SageMaker AI Serverless Model Customization supports many hyperparameters. In this section, we explain a few important ones, informed by the series of experiments that we ran across multiple hyperparameter configurations within the previously mentioned code generation task. While effective values will vary by task, the patterns observed across these experiments provide useful starting points when configuring FFT model customization jobs. For more information about the hyperparameters that you can configure before launching an FFT model customization job, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/customizing-models-sft.html#customizing-models-sft-hyperparameters" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;hyperparameters documentation page&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="learning_rate"&gt;1. learning_rate&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In full fine-tuning, the learning rate controls how aggressively every weight is updated. Unlike LoRA, where only small adapter matrices change, an FFT learning rate that is too high permanently overwrites pretrained knowledge. In our experiments, the following LR × dataset-size risk map shows that an LR of 2e-5 on a large dataset (over 100 thousand) was the safe zone.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-9.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-9.png" alt="Learning rate by dataset-size risk map showing 2e-5 on a 100K-plus dataset as the safe zone" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 9: Learning rate and dataset-size risk map for full fine-tuning&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="max_epochs"&gt;2. max_epochs&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Epoch count in FFT is fundamentally a forgetting question, not a loss-curve question. Training loss will keep falling and your held-out validation will look healthy because it shares a distribution with training data, but capabilities on everything you didn’t train on quietly degrade. The LLM-as-Judge correctness score rose to 90.0 percent at 3 epochs, and extending beyond 3 epochs degraded every variant we experimented with.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="global_batch_size"&gt;3. global_batch_size&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Batch size determines how many examples contribute to each gradient step. At a fixed learning rate, smaller batches produce more noisy updates that behave much like a higher learning rate, increasing forgetting risk. Larger batches reduce noise but also reduce the number of optimization steps, potentially underfitting.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-10.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-10.png" alt="Effect of global batch size on model quality at a fixed learning rate" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 10: Effect of global batch size on model quality&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="catastrophic-forgetting-risk"&gt;Catastrophic forgetting risk&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;FFT modifies every parameter in the model, including the parameters that encode general knowledge acquired during pre-training. When fine-tuning pushes these parameters too aggressively toward a narrow domain, the model can “forget” its broader capabilities, a phenomenon known as catastrophic forgetting. We tested for this systematically using MMLU, measuring general knowledge retention across all our fine-tuning configurations. A baseline configuration with warmup=5 percent, batch size 8, and LR=2e-5 best preserved general knowledge (MMLU), as shown in the following figure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-11.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-11.png" alt="MMLU general-knowledge retention across fine-tuning hyperparameter configurations" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 11: MMLU general-knowledge retention across hyperparameter configurations&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Hence, it is important to note that FFT isn’t universally superior to LoRA, and its effectiveness is extremely sensitive to hyperparameter choices. For example, in our experiments, both FFT and LoRA underperformed the base model on MATH exact match, underscoring the challenge of preserving reasoning capabilities during domain-specific fine-tuning. Aggressive learning rates (LR ≥ 5e-5) in FFT proved particularly destructive, causing MATH scores to plummet to 2–3 percent and MMLU accuracy to drop to 3.6 percent, clear evidence of catastrophic forgetting. Additionally, LoRA slightly outperformed FFT on the LLM-as-Judge metric (81.5 percent compared to 79.5 percent), demonstrating that parameter-efficient methods can still hold an edge on subjective quality assessments. These findings reinforce that the choice between FFT and LoRA should be guided by careful experimentation, dataset characteristics, and a clear understanding of the trade-offs involved.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/customizing-models-fft.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Full Fine-Tuning on Amazon SageMaker AI Serverless Model Customization&lt;/a&gt;, your model can fully absorb domain-specific knowledge when task complexity and data scale demand it. If your use case requires deep adaptation, such as specialized reasoning, code generation, or domain-specific language understanding, and you have sufficient high-quality training data, FFT delivers measurably stronger results.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can get started with FFT on &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/home" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker AI Serverless Model Customization&lt;/a&gt; with serverless training, built-in evaluation, and without infrastructure or compute capacity planning, so you can focus on your data and hyperparameter choices. You can use the &lt;a href="https://sagemaker.readthedocs.io/en/stable/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Python SDK&lt;/a&gt; to configure and launch your training and evaluation jobs programmatically. You can see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/model-customize-open-weight.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;documentation for supported base models&lt;/a&gt; available for Full Fine-Tuning on Serverless Model Customization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;About the author&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;footer&gt; 
 &lt;div class="blog-author-box" style="padding-top: 2.0em"&gt; 
  &lt;div class="blog-author-image" style="margin-right: 1.0em"&gt;
   &lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignnone size-full" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21644-12.jpg" alt="Sandeep Raveesh-Babu" width="100" height="133"&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt; 
  &lt;h3 class="lb-h4"&gt;Sandeep Raveesh-Babu&lt;/h3&gt; 
  &lt;p style="overflow: hidden"&gt;Sandeep is a GenAI Specialist Solutions Architect at AWS. He works with customers through their LLM training, LLM inference, and GenAI observability. He focuses on product development helping AWS build and solve industry challenges in the generative AI space. You can connect with Sandeep on &lt;a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/sandeep-raveesh-750aa630/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LinkedIn&lt;/a&gt; to learn about generative AI solutions.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;/footer&gt;</content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Exa boosts embedding throughput by 50% to index billions of documents on AWS AI chips</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/exa-boosts-embedding-throughput-by-50-to-index-billions-of-documents-on-aws-ai-chips/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Summer Devlin]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 18 Nov 2026 23:58:22 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AWS Inferentia]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AWS Trainium]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">7561fc3d330a372ef121d07f7930d0f64fd7d3eb</guid>

					<description>Exa's neural search engine is backed by an in-house vector database, so the unit economics of embedding tens of billions of web documents drive the business. This post shares how Exa optimized embedding inference on AWS Neuron hardware (Inferentia2 and Trainium2) with custom NKI kernels, reaching a 47% gain in performance and cost per million documents.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;At &lt;a href="https://exa.ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Exa&lt;/a&gt;, we are building one of the few true web indexes by aggregating and serving knowledge from across the web, along with private and synthetic sources. We optimize the index for semantic search, grounding agents and humans in true, high-quality data. We use multi-tower semantic machine learning (ML) models to embed web data and query data into the same vector space, so users can express complex queries in natural language alongside traditional keyword search. The keystone technology behind this is an in-house vector database. It must ingest as much high-quality web data as possible and embed all of it. The unit economics of embedding therefore underlie everything we do. At the scale of tens or hundreds of billions of documents, finding the hardware that delivers the best performance per dollar isn’t a tuning detail. Any per-document cost improvement compounds into a significant number.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post details how we optimized our embedding infrastructure for cheaper, more flexible inference workloads on AWS Neuron hardware (&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/machine-learning/inferentia/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Inferentia2&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/machine-learning/trainium/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Trainium2&lt;/a&gt;), and how an agentic development workflow drove the optimization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Inference runs on an asynchronous pipeline, but it must be high-volume and fast to serve recent data quickly and shrink the time between discovery and serving.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/13/ML-22138-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/13/ML-22138-1-1.png" alt="Exa stack showing web data flowing from crawlers through parse, moderate, and embed stages into a vector database" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: A simplified view of the Exa stack&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every structured web document we rank as high enough quality must be embedded before it can be served. This figure shows how raw web data moves from Crawlers into an asynchronous pipeline of Parse, Moderate and filter, then Embed, with the result served from a vector database.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="embedding-documents-at-scale"&gt;Embedding documents at scale&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Our experience with the Neuron hardware family began with a backfill. Moving to a superior document model meant re-embedding our existing corpus. We selected 30 billion high-priority documents for re-embedding. A migration like this temporarily doubles the embedding load, which made it exactly the right moment to re-evaluate our hardware choices.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/13/ML-22138-2-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/13/ML-22138-2-1.png" alt="Two parallel embedding pipelines sharing crawl and parse stages, doubling the inference load during the backfill" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: The doubled inference load from the backfill&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The preceding figure shows two parallel pipelines sharing the crawl and parse stages, one embedding with the live model into the live index and one re-embedding priority documents with the new model into the new index, which is what doubles the load. This doubling was a clear trigger to reconsider our hardware and optimization choices.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="optimization-journey"&gt;Optimization journey&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS Inferentia2 figures refer to performance on the smallest instance type, an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/instance-types/inf2/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Inferentia2 xlarge instance&lt;/a&gt; (&lt;code&gt;inf2.xlarge&lt;/code&gt;), with one chip and two &lt;a href="https://awsdocs-neuron.readthedocs-hosted.com/en/latest/about-neuron/arch/neuron-hardware/neuron-core-v2.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NeuronCores&lt;/a&gt;. Our workload runs on a single NeuronCore, so we run Kubernetes pods that pack onto larger instances without leaving any cores idle, giving us container homogeneity across the embedding fleet. The same process runs on a Trainium2 NeuronCore and scales the same way through data parallelism. Our initial Inferentia2 implementation was a straightforward data-parallel, FP16 representation of the model. From that baseline, it became a march to push throughput (docs/sec) as high as possible, using the Neuron SDK and support from the AWS Trainium team. The following chart shows the throughput gains at each milestone.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 477px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/13/ML-22138-3-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/13/ML-22138-3-1.png" alt="Chart of throughput gains across optimization milestones, rising to about 1.5 times the baseline" width="467"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Throughput improvement across optimization milestones&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Starting from the naive compile, a progressive series of optimizations ultimately delivered roughly a 1.5× throughput improvement. The first was weight layout optimization, which eliminates unnecessary transposes by aligning the weight layout with the scheduling and layout of the broader model. We also enabled autocast, letting the compiler downcast matrix multiplications to lower precision while preserving dynamic range. The downcast outputs met our correctness gate of 0.999 cosine similarity against the reference (see &lt;a href="#agentic-methodology"&gt;Agentic methodology&lt;/a&gt;), so the accuracy impact was within our acceptance threshold.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="nki-kernels"&gt;NKI kernels&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;a href="https://awsdocs-neuron.readthedocs-hosted.com/en/v2.33.0/nki/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Neuron Kernel Interface&lt;/a&gt; (NKI) library provides optimized kernels for common model layers and operations. These pre-built kernels deliver immediate gains on common features, and we used them to quickly remove the most straightforward sources of inefficiency. A drop-in attention kernel gave us the next bump in performance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="memory-layout-optimization-a-fused-swiglu-mlp-kernel"&gt;Memory layout optimization: A fused SwiGLU MLP kernel&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The next bottleneck was memory spill. Profiles from the &lt;a href="https://awsdocs-neuron.readthedocs-hosted.com/en/latest/tools/neuron-explorer/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Neuron Explorer&lt;/a&gt; consistently showed large amounts of spill and reload from high-bandwidth memory (HBM), which kept the Tensor Engine from being fully utilized through the forward pass. Using a drop-in MLP kernel as a steppingstone to profile the shortcomings of the pre-built implementation, we wrote a custom weight-stationary fused SwiGLU MLP kernel that eliminated the HBM stalls.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We used the higher-level NKI language for layout operations, loops, and memory operations, and only dropped into the lower-level NKI ISA for specific tensor operations. Keeping most of the kernel in the higher-level language makes it more understandable for engineers, and easier to debug.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="flops-reduction"&gt;FLOPS reduction&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At this point we hit a wall. Per-accelerator throughput measured with the other on-chip core idle was significantly higher than what we saw with both cores running simultaneously. That gap meant our theoretical data-parallel throughput should have been well above our realized aggregate performance, which pointed to contention between the cores.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Profiling and experimentation showed that our workload wasn’t yet saturating the Tensor Engine. The remaining headroom was ours to claim by making the computation leaner. The clearest path left was to reduce the overall FLOPS needed to produce our results by eliminating waste. We rewrote the attention kernel around the exact shapes in our model to cut FLOPS spent on padding. This is where kernel customization really paid off. Generic kernels are built to handle any shape, so they can’t take advantage of the specific shapes in a given architecture. We wrung more performance out of attention by tailoring the implementation to the real shape of our model’s head architecture. We also folded attention operations into single matmuls where the generic kernel used multiple instruction steps. That cut the inner-loop operations needed to compute the same values, which reduced HBM round trips and produced the final significant speedup.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In total, our final version was a 47% improvement in performance and cost per 1 million documents embedded, against our initial baseline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="agentic-methodology"&gt;Agentic methodology&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agents developed all of these optimizations inside a simple three part harness that lets them verify correctness, measure performance and profile the workload to find the next direction. Using &lt;a href="https://flyte.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Flyte&lt;/a&gt;, an ML ops workflow platform, each agent runs its own workflow. The workflow compiles a &lt;a href="https://awsdocs-neuron.readthedocs-hosted.com/en/latest/compiler/neuronx-cc/how-to-generate-neff.html#neuronx-cc-generate-neff-how-to" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NEFF&lt;/a&gt; Neuron executable and checks a forward pass against a baseline dataset, which must reach 0.999 cosine similarity to the canonical model. It then profiles one forward pass and runs a 30 minute saturation benchmark on real hardware.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The harness supports agents at different costs. Lightweight models handle tedious work like grid searching compiler parameters, and correctness checks prevent drift. Reasoning agents test complex hypotheses and find bottlenecks, while the strongest code generators write custom kernels to fix them. Matching each agent to its task keeps costs in check, and validation keeps capable agents from drifting. Because many agents don’t know the latest features and syntax, &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-neuron/neuron-agentic-development/tree/main" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;neuron agentic development skills&lt;/a&gt; also helped.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-benefits-of-flexible-compute"&gt;The benefits of flexible compute&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;So far, we have optimized Inferentia2 for the US West (Oregon) Region (&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/global-infrastructure/regions_az/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;us-west-2&lt;/a&gt;), but tuning one hardware family in one Region has hard limits. Spot and on demand availability vary by Region, and organizations with many consumers compete with themselves. Locking into one Region or instance type almost guarantees a compute shortage, and possibly degraded service.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Exa schedules compute across multiple Regions from one Kubernetes control plane. Our scheduler compares performance per dollar across instance types and packs pods onto the cheapest nodes, rescheduling as embedding demand shifts. Each hardware type we optimize for brings us closer to the global optimum. This is why our Inferentia2 work carried over directly to Trainium2. Shifting hardware profiles lets us free up nodes other teams need or absorb their unused reserved compute. For a long lived model, optimizing across hardware pays off enormously.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At the scale Exa operates, extracting maximum cost-performance from a flexible pool of hardware is what matters. Adding Neuron hardware to that pool and optimizing it with NKI has proven to be a major source of long-term cost savings.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;
