&lt;/footer&gt;</content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Introducing the Amazon SageMaker JumpStart Model Leaderboard</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-the-amazon-sagemaker-jumpstart-model-leaderboard/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Dan Ferguson]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 18 Nov 2026 22:31:04 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker JumpStart]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Foundational (100)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">179a030b28535518f068b87e596fbaedc2dd8926</guid>

					<description>The Amazon SageMaker JumpStart Model Leaderboard compares 70+ foundation models side by side on quality, speed, and cost, directly in SageMaker Studio. Learn what the leaderboard shows, how AWS benchmarks models, and how to go from a shortlist to a deployed model.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Amazon SageMaker JumpStart gives you hundreds of open-weight foundation models you can deploy quickly. That choice is powerful, but it also creates a real question: Which model should you start with? Comparing models usually means reading model cards one at a time and hunting for benchmark scores that different sources published under different conditions. Then you still must guess how each model will perform, and what it will cost, for your use case.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Today we are launching the &lt;strong&gt;Amazon SageMaker JumpStart Model Leaderboard&lt;/strong&gt;, a new way to compare &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/home#/launch?source=jumpstart&amp;amp;page=jumpstart" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;JumpStart models&lt;/a&gt; side by side on quality, speed, and cost, directly in Amazon SageMaker Studio. In this post, we explain what the leaderboard shows, define the key terms in plain language, and walk through how to go from a shortlist to a deployed model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-is-the-jumpstart-model-leaderboard"&gt;What is the JumpStart Model Leaderboard?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The leaderboard is a new view of the JumpStart base models page in SageMaker Studio, right next to the Categories and All models views. It uses the same search bar and filters you already know, and it opens on a leaderboard of large language models (LLMs). You see rankings immediately without answering setup questions first. For each model, it shows two kinds of information:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Quality scores&lt;/strong&gt; on three public benchmarks (BIG-Bench Hard, GPQA Diamond, and MMLU-Pro) that AWS runs itself, using the same evaluation setup for every model so the numbers are comparable.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Performance and cost metrics&lt;/strong&gt; that AWS measures by sending a standardized test workload to an endpoint using each JumpStart deployment configuration, so you see how fast a model responds and what it costs to run.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because both kinds of numbers come from the same place and the same method, you can make a like-for-like comparison instead of stitching together data from different websites. At launch, the leaderboard covers &lt;strong&gt;70+ models&lt;/strong&gt; from providers including Meta, Google, Qwen, IBM, and Arcee, with more added over time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshot shows the leaderboard in SageMaker Studio, with benchmark scores, performance metrics, and estimated cost for each model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22496-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22496-1-1.png" alt="SageMaker JumpStart Model Leaderboard in SageMaker Studio showing benchmark scores, performance metrics, and estimated cost per model" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Leaderboard overview in SageMaker Studio&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="key-concepts"&gt;Key concepts&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This section defines the terms the leaderboard uses: the quality benchmarks, the use case profiles and optimization goals that shape the performance numbers, and the performance metrics themselves.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="quality-benchmarks"&gt;Quality benchmarks&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A benchmark is a standardized test: a fixed set of questions with known answers. Running every model through the same test gives you a score you can compare. At launch, the leaderboard reports three reasoning benchmarks for LLMs:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Benchmark&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What it measures, in plain terms&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;BIG-Bench Hard (BBH)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A set of challenging tasks that require multi-step reasoning, such as logical deduction, tracking objects, and multi-step arithmetic.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;GPQA Diamond&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Graduate-level science questions that are difficult to answer by searching the web. A good signal of deep reasoning.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;MMLU-Pro&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Broad knowledge and reasoning across many subjects, such as science, law, and engineering, using harder multiple-choice questions.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Higher is better for all three. The leaderboard shows each benchmark separately rather than blending them into one number. That keeps the results transparent: You can weigh the benchmarks that matter for your application and check each score against the public definition of the benchmark.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="use-case-profiles-and-optimization-goals"&gt;Use case profiles and optimization goals&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Speed and cost depend on your workload and on how the model is deployed. Two menus above the leaderboard control which performance and cost numbers you see:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use case profile.&lt;/strong&gt; Select the profile closest to your workload from the View benchmarks for your use case menu. The default is Max Context. The profile changes only the performance and cost metrics. Quality benchmark scores are the same for every profile.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Optimization goal.&lt;/strong&gt; For each profile, a model can have several deployment configurations, and the leaderboard shows metrics for one at a time. Choose which one with the Optimized for menu. The default is Balanced.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each optimization goal picks a different configuration:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Optimization goal&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Configuration shown&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Balanced&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The configuration with the lowest estimated cost per token. Because cost per token depends on both instance price and throughput, this balances what you pay per hour against how fast the instance generates tokens.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cost optimized&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The configuration with the lowest hourly instance price. Latency can be higher and throughput lower than with other goals.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Latency optimized&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The configuration with the lowest time to first token.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Throughput optimized&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The configuration with the highest throughput. These configurations can use speculative decoding.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="performance-metrics"&gt;Performance metrics&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metric&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What it tells you&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Time to first token (TTFT)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;How long a user waits before the model starts responding. Lower is better, and it matters most for chat.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Throughput per request (tokens/s)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;How many tokens the model generates per second. Higher is better, and it matters for batch and high-volume workloads.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Instance cost ($/hr)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The estimated cost per hour given a specified instance type.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A note on cost:&lt;/strong&gt; Estimated cost reflects SageMaker instance cost only, based on the on-demand hourly rate for the configuration’s default instance type. It does not include data transfer, storage, inference component costs, or discounts such as Reserved Instances or AWS Savings Plans. Your actual bill will vary. Use the &lt;a href="https://calculator.aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Pricing Calculator&lt;/a&gt; for billing estimates.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-we-benchmark"&gt;How we benchmark&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Published benchmark scores often come from different teams using different prompts, settings, and hardware, which makes them hard to compare. We took a different approach:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;One method for every model.&lt;/strong&gt; AWS runs each benchmark with the same evaluation harness and settings across all models on the leaderboard.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Measured on SageMaker.&lt;/strong&gt; Performance and cost come from sending a standardized test workload to an endpoint deployed with each JumpStart deployment configuration, the same configurations you can launch.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For full details, including prompts, generation settings, and how configurations are measured, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/studio-jumpstart.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;JumpStart leaderboard methodology&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="walkthrough-finding-a-model"&gt;Walkthrough: Finding a model&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following steps show how to go from browsing to a deployed model. You need access to SageMaker Studio and permission to deploy JumpStart models.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-open-the-leaderboard"&gt;Step 1: Open the leaderboard&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In SageMaker Studio, open the Models page, then select the Leaderboard view. The leaderboard keeps the same search bar and filters as the other views, and it has its own URL. You can bookmark it or share a direct link with your team.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshot shows the Models page with the Leaderboard view selected.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22496-2-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22496-2-1.png" alt="JumpStart base models page in SageMaker Studio with the Leaderboard view selected" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: JumpStart base models page with the Leaderboard view selected&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-narrow-the-list-with-filters"&gt;Step 2: Narrow the list with filters&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use the same search and filters you already use elsewhere in JumpStart to keep only the models that fit your requirements. Common filters include license, provider, and input and output modality. Start with hard requirements, for example a license that allows commercial use, before you look at scores.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshot shows the leaderboard filtered by input modality, output modality, and license.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22496-3-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22496-3-1.png" alt="Leaderboard filtered by input modality, output modality, and license" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Leaderboard with filters applied&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-choose-a-use-case-profile-and-optimization-goal"&gt;Step 3: Choose a use case profile and optimization goal&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Above the leaderboard, select the profile closest to your workload from the View benchmarks for your use case menu (default: Max Context). Then choose a goal from the Optimized for menu (default: Balanced). For a chatbot your customers interact with, Latency optimized is a sensible start. For overnight document processing, Throughput optimized or Cost optimized may fit better. The performance and cost columns update, and quality scores stay the same.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshots show the View benchmarks for your use case and Optimized for menus above the leaderboard.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22496-4-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22496-4-1.png" alt="View benchmarks for your use case menu above the leaderboard" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: View benchmarks for your use case menu&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22496-5-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22496-5-1.png" alt="Optimized for menu above the leaderboard" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Optimized for menu&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-read-the-heatmap-and-customize-columns"&gt;Step 4: Read the heatmap and customize columns&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each column has a blue heatmap, so the top-performing models stand out at a glance: the deeper the blue, the stronger the result in that column. Sort any benchmark column to rank models by the metric you care about most. Use the column settings (gear icon) to hide metrics you don’t need, and scroll horizontally to see more. The model name columns stay frozen as you scroll, and a fade at the edge shows when more columns are available.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshot shows the heatmap and the column settings menu.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22496-6-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22496-6-1.png" alt="Leaderboard heatmap with the column settings menu open" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: Heatmap and column settings&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-5-deploy-or-customize-the-model"&gt;Step 5: Deploy or customize the model&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Choose a model to open it. From there, you have options to deploy the model or, for models that support serverless customization, to customize it. If you deploy, choose the same use case and optimization goal you compared on the leaderboard. Each metric applies to the configuration it was measured on, and a different configuration can change quality, latency, throughput, and cost.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshot shows a model page with the Deploy and Customize options.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22496-7-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22496-7-1.png" alt="JumpStart model page showing the Deploy and Customize options" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: Model page with the Deploy and Customize options&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="example-picking-a-model-for-a-chat-assistant"&gt;Example: Picking a model for a chat assistant&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Suppose you are building an internal help-desk assistant. Employees ask questions and expect fast, accurate answers that follow a set response format. You need a commercially licensed model and want to keep costs predictable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After filtering by license and choosing a chat-like use case profile with Latency optimized, your shortlist might look like this:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;BBH&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;MMLU-Pro&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;TTFT (s)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Throughput (tokens/s)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Instance cost ($/hr)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model A (large)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;95.9&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;87.2&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2.33&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;37&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$37.66&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model B (medium)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;94.2&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;84.9&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0.98&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;44&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$5.75&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model C (small)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;77.4&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;66.1&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0.27&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;61&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$2.61&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Reading the table, Model A has the highest knowledge score but also the highest cost and the slowest first response. Model B scores almost as well, holds up on multi-step reasoning, and runs on a smaller, cheaper instance. For a help desk, where reasoning through a question and responding quickly matter more than the last few points of general knowledge, Model B is the better starting point. A bigger model is not always the better choice, and the leaderboard makes that trade-off visible in seconds.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;From there, you would deploy Model B, test it with real questions from your help desk, and keep Model C in mind as a lower-cost option if quality holds up.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="tips-and-things-to-know"&gt;Tips and things to know&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use the leaderboard to build a shortlist, not to make the final call.&lt;/strong&gt; Benchmarks measure general ability. Always evaluate your top two or three models on your own data and prompts before going to production.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Filter first, then rank.&lt;/strong&gt; Requirements like license and input or output modality rule models in or out. Scores only help you order what is left.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Compare models within a benchmark.&lt;/strong&gt; Each benchmark has its own difficulty, so a score on one benchmark isn’t directly comparable with a score on another.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Account for model type.&lt;/strong&gt; Reasoning and non-reasoning models are evaluated with different generation parameters, and GPQA Diamond scores for reasoning models are averaged over 10 passes. Comparisons between the two types are approximate.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Check the deployment configuration.&lt;/strong&gt; Each metric applies to the configuration it was measured on. Compare models on the configuration you plan to deploy.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Treat close scores as ties.&lt;/strong&gt; Small differences can fall within normal run-to-run variation and don’t mean one model is meaningfully better. Look for clear gaps.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Coverage is growing.&lt;/strong&gt; Not every JumpStart model has benchmark scores yet, and launch benchmarks focus on LLMs. A model without a score is not a weak model. It simply has not been benchmarked yet.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="whats-next"&gt;What’s next&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We’re continuing to expand the leaderboard. Areas we’re exploring include:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Private hub support,&lt;/strong&gt; so organizations that use curated JumpStart private hubs can see a leaderboard for their approved models.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;More task leaderboards&lt;/strong&gt; beyond LLM text generation, such as object detection, text embeddings, and image understanding.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;More models and benchmarks&lt;/strong&gt; as new models are added to JumpStart.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The SageMaker JumpStart Model Leaderboard brings quality, speed, and cost together in one place, measured consistently on SageMaker. Instead of comparing numbers from different sources, you can filter to the models that fit your requirements, compare them on the configuration you plan to run, and deploy with a few clicks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, open the Models page in SageMaker Studio and select the Leaderboard view. To learn more, see the following resources:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/studio-jumpstart.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;JumpStart leaderboard methodology&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/studio-jumpstart.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker JumpStart documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/use-case-based-deployments-on-sagemaker-jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Use-case based deployments on SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/new-serverless-customization-in-amazon-sagemaker-ai-accelerates-model-fine-tuning/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Customize a model with SageMaker serverless customization&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Open the leaderboard, shortlist two or three models for your workload, and deploy them to test with your own prompts before you choose one for production.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How Airbnb uses cost per task to operate coding agents on Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-airbnb-uses-cost-per-task-to-operate-coding-agents-on-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Alan Yao]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 18 Nov 2026 21:48:19 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">894cf5f974d052ae129273f3503e15ca222b6c15</guid>

					<description>Airbnb runs evals on real engineering tasks and uses Amazon Bedrock observability to choose models for its coding agents and control production costs. This post shows how cost per task guides model selection, why production spending can differ from eval results, and how to monitor latency, manage quotas, and attribute spend.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock provides access to foundation models from multiple providers through a common API, with AWS managing the model infrastructure. It integrates with AWS access controls and monitoring, and processes prompts, code, and model responses within AWS.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As coding agents move into production, changes in models and usage can affect latency, capacity, and cost. Amazon Bedrock runtime metrics in Amazon CloudWatch help teams investigate latency increases, throttling, and changes in token consumption. Evaluations, or evals, complement these operational metrics by assessing the quality of an agent’s work on a fixed set of representative tasks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we show how Airbnb evaluates models for coding agents and monitors their use on Amazon Bedrock. We explain how cost per task guides model selection and how production data helps us revisit those choices as usage changes. We also describe the runtime signals used to investigate latency, throttling, and spend.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At Airbnb, thousands of engineers and engineering-adjacent people use AirChat, our internal tool for running coding agents, every week. The agents send their model requests through our large language model (LLM) gateway, which runs on Amazon Bedrock. Bedrock processes Airbnb’s code, prompts, and model responses within AWS. Eight new models came out in about five weeks. By mid-October, about two thirds of the requests our engineers sent through coding agents went to those new models.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each release can change output tokens, cost, latency and capacity at once, and the benchmark charts published with a launch don’t show how a model handles our engineers’ work. So we run two systems side by side. Before a rollout, we run evals on our own tasks to measure each model’s score and cost per task. Cost per task is the list-price cost of all the tokens an agent uses to finish one task.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After a rollout, observability shows us what changed in production. When production usage doesn’t match the eval results, we find out why.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can diagnose most production problems with a few signals in the &lt;code&gt;AWS/Bedrock&lt;/code&gt; namespace in CloudWatch:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Latency: &lt;code&gt;InvocationLatency&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;TimeToFirstToken&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;OutputTokenCount&lt;/code&gt;, and output tokens per second (OTPS), which you derive.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Throttling: &lt;code&gt;InvocationThrottles&lt;/code&gt;, plus the tokens each request reserves against your quota, which you publish yourself. Bedrock also publishes &lt;code&gt;EstimatedTPMQuotaUsage&lt;/code&gt;, which measures your estimated tokens per minute (TPM) quota consumption, including cache write tokens and output burndown multipliers, across all inference APIs (&lt;code&gt;Converse&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;InvokeModel&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ConverseStream&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;InvokeModelWithResponseStream&lt;/code&gt;). You can use this metric to set proactive alarms before reaching your quota limit, track your quota consumption across your models, and request further quota increases before usage is rate limited.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Cost: &lt;code&gt;InputTokenCount&lt;/code&gt; (uncached input), &lt;code&gt;CacheReadInputTokenCount&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;CacheWriteInputTokenCount&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;OutputTokenCount&lt;/code&gt;, each priced at its own rate. Cache reads cost less than uncached input, and cache writes can cost more depending on the model. A lower cache hit rate can increase cost per request.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On top of these metrics, we add cost per task. Our evals measure a score and a cost per task for every model and every reasoning effort we allow. Reasoning effort is a setting that controls how much a model reasons before it responds. We use the results to choose each default, which is the model and effort that our coding tools use unless an engineer picks another, and each effort ceiling, which is the highest effort we allow for a model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="architecture"&gt;Architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Engineers run AirChat on their AirDev workspaces, which are personal development machines in the cloud. They can also hand a task to AirChat Remote, which runs cloud agents in separate cloud workspaces while the engineer does something else. Both paths go through our LLM gateway to Amazon Bedrock.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 1 shows how AirChat and AirChat Remote reach Amazon Bedrock through our gateway. It also shows how evals and production monitoring guide model defaults and effort ceilings.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22452-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22452-1.png" alt="AirChat and AirChat Remote reach Amazon Bedrock through Airbnb’s gateway, with CloudWatch metrics and eval results setting model defaults and effort ceilings" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Coding agents and cloud agents call Amazon Bedrock through our LLM gateway. Our usage records and CloudWatch metrics show real use, and we use eval results to set defaults and effort ceilings&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gateway records the model, client, team, and user for each request. We use these records only in aggregate, for cost and capacity planning. CloudWatch supplies token counts, latency, errors, and throttles. We compare those production signals with our eval scores and cost per task to decide when defaults or effort ceilings need to change.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On the AWS side, you need:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Access to the models in Amazon Bedrock you plan to run and evaluate. For model availability by AWS Region, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-region-compatibility.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Regional availability by models&lt;/a&gt; in the Amazon Bedrock User Guide.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Streaming calls through &lt;code&gt;ConverseStream&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;InvokeModelWithResponseStream&lt;/code&gt;. Bedrock publishes &lt;code&gt;TimeToFirstToken&lt;/code&gt; only for the streaming APIs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Model invocation logging, turned on in every AWS Region you call. It’s off by default. The logs can hold full prompts and responses along with the caller’s identity, so restrict who can read them and set a retention period.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tagged IAM roles for your callers, with cost allocation tags activated. Activate them before you start collecting, because activation doesn’t backfill earlier costs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You also need three things of your own:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A set of tasks from your own engineering work. Give each one a starting repository state, the prompt an engineer would write and a grader you trust. A grader is the program or model that scores the agent’s work. We build our tasks from real code changes, the review comments on those changes and the decisions that the original ticket didn’t spell out. Step 2 shows one.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A gateway or proxy in front of Bedrock that records the model, the client, the team, the user and the token counts for each request.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A table of public list prices for every model you run, with a date on each entry, so you can price eval tokens and production tokens on the same basis.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before rollout, define what the agents can access and which actions need human review. Run code in isolated workspaces and test generated changes before merging. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/guardrails.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; can help filter harmful content and sensitive information in prompts and responses. For responses based on supplied reference material, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/guardrails-contextual-grounding-check.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;contextual grounding checks&lt;/a&gt; can flag unsupported claims. Test these controls on your own tasks and include their latency and cost in your measurements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="walkthrough"&gt;Walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Start by measuring what a complete task costs. Then use those results to choose model defaults, check production behavior, manage capacity, and attribute spend.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-what-does-one-finished-task-cost-you"&gt;Step 1: What does one finished task cost you?&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A coding agent takes many requests to finish one task, and on each request the model re-reads the context so far. So we price the whole task.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Bedrock reports four kinds of tokens for each request: uncached input, cache reads, cache writes and output. In an eval run we price all four for every request and add up the requests in the task. Every cost in this post uses the public list prices dated for that run. We record the average cost next to the score for every model, reasoning effort and agent client we test, and the average includes low-scoring attempts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Reasoning effort changes both score and cost. On one model we tested, moving from low to high effort raised the score by 0.8 points. Cost per task was 2.5 times as high. We use these results to set effort ceilings, which the gateway enforces.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-how-fast-can-you-evaluate-a-new-model"&gt;Step 2: How fast can you evaluate a new model?&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On the afternoon a new model came out, a single request to our agents started an eval campaign, which runs the new model through all our task suites. A suite is a fixed set of tasks of one type, and we have seven. Agents spread hundreds of tasks across the suites and several cloud workspaces, and each result appeared as soon as a grader scored it. First results arrived within hours. By the next morning, every suite had a score and a cost per task, and we had what we needed for a deployment decision for the entire org.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;That speed depends on tasks that are already written down. Each task is a folder that holds everything one run needs. Here is a trimmed task from our machine learning work:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;honest-holdout-bakeoff/
  instruction.md     what the agent is told
  task.toml          time limit, CPUs and memory
  environment/       Dockerfile for the sandbox
  tests/test.sh      runs the grader and writes the reward
  tests/grader.py    the checks
  solution/          a reference solution that must pass the grader

instruction.md (excerpt):
  We have two candidate models for a prediction task, and we need to
  know which one to ship.
  ...
  Then answer the question we actually have to decide: is either
  candidate worth rolling out over the system already in production?&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The grader recomputes the right answer from frozen data on every run, and the agent earns a reward of 1 only if every check passes. To test one model at every effort, list each effort as its own agent in a job config:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;job_name: model-a-effort-sweep
n_attempts: 1                # one attempt per task at each effort
datasets:
  - path: tasks/ml           # each task brings its own grader
agents:
  - name: model-a-low
    model_name: vendor/model-a
    kwargs: {reasoning_effort: low}
  - name: model-a-medium
    model_name: vendor/model-a
    kwargs: {reasoning_effort: medium}
  - name: model-a-high
    model_name: vendor/model-a
    kwargs: {reasoning_effort: high}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Both examples use the formats of &lt;a href="https://github.com/harbor-framework/harbor" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Harbor&lt;/a&gt;, an open-source framework for running agent evals in sandboxes. It’s one option if you’re starting out.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Running the evals can be optimized. Writing the rubric, the scoring guide that a grader follows, is the hardest part. Our long tasks run over several rounds, with a simulated reviewer pushing back between rounds. A model grader scores each run on eight weighted dimensions. Each dimension gets a score from 0 to 3.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;Correctness of delivery   25     Judgment                 10
Verification quality      15     Evidence and reporting   10
Autonomy                  15     Readability              10
Design and workload fit   10     Operational discipline    5

Ceiling: if the reviewer had to rescue the agent repeatedly
(autonomy 0 or 0.5), the total is capped at 25 of 100.&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because of ceilings like this one, an agent that needed rescuing can’t score well on polish alone.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-what-do-you-watch-after-a-rollout"&gt;Step 3: What do you watch after a rollout?&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When latency rises, chart three signals before you open a support case: &lt;code&gt;TimeToFirstToken&lt;/code&gt; for the wait before generation starts, &lt;code&gt;OutputTokenCount&lt;/code&gt; for response length, and OTPS for generation speed. Bedrock has no native OTPS metric. You derive it with metric math, as the Amazon &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/monitoring-runtime-otps.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Bedrock User Guide&lt;/a&gt; describes:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;OTPS = OutputTokenCount / (InvocationLatency - TimeToFirstToken) * 1000&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the example from our session, latency rose 40 percent while time to first token and OTPS stayed flat. Someone had changed the system prompt to ask for more thorough answers, and output tokens per request rose 50 percent. The model generated at the same speed and wrote longer responses. Treat prompt changes and model upgrades as deploys, and mark them on your dashboard.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each week we put the CloudWatch runtime metrics next to the usage records from our gateway for each model. For cost and capacity planning, we also look at usage in aggregate: cost per request and the average number of requests per user each week. A new model can move both. After every rollout, we compare them with what we expected from the eval’s cost per task.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-the-evals-looked-good.-what-did-production-show"&gt;Step 4: The evals looked good. What did production show?&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One model, which we call model A, looked good in our evals. On our coding tasks, it scored more than three points above our previous best model. Cost per task was about the same, and the same grader scored every model. We rolled it out, capped effort at high, and asked people to use low or medium for most work.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A few weeks after launch, we priced model A’s production usage at list price and looked at it in aggregate. Average weekly spend per user was several times higher on model A than on the model before it. Each model A request cost about three times as much, and the average model A user sent about three times as many requests.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Output tokens made up only a tenth of a request’s cost. Most of the cost went to cached context, which the model re-read on every request. Model A could work on its own for a long time, so people started long, self-directed sessions with it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The eval measured the model on a task. Production measured how people chose to work with it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Later, a newer model scored higher than model A on the same tasks and grader, at about a sixth of its cost per task. We moved model A’s users to it with a configuration change.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We then checked the change in aggregate on the same user group, the heavy users of model A. In their first full week on the newer model, the group sent about the same number of requests to their main model, and the group’s spend on that model fell to about a seventh.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-5-why-you-get-throttled-and-where-traffic-goes-next"&gt;Step 5: Why you get throttled, and where traffic goes next&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On the &lt;code&gt;bedrock-runtime&lt;/code&gt; endpoint, Bedrock checks your tokens-per-minute quota when a request arrives, before the model writes anything. It reserves your uncached input tokens and your cache writes, plus &lt;code&gt;max_tokens&lt;/code&gt;, the most output the request is allowed. It adjusts that reservation as the model generates and returns the unused part when the request settles. Cache reads never count against the quota, so a request can be cheap against your quota and expensive on your bill. Output tokens settle at a burndown rate that is 1:1 on most models and several times that on some current models, so check &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/quotas-token-burndown.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;your model’s rate&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A dashboard of billed tokens can show room while requests are being throttled. In our session’s example, the quota was 1 million tokens per minute and traffic was 300 requests per minute. The dashboard showed about 30 percent of quota. After applying a 5:1 output-token burndown rate, completed requests consumed about 77 percent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Admission uses a different calculation. Each request initially reserved 600 uncached input tokens plus a &lt;code&gt;max_tokens&lt;/code&gt; of 4,096, for a total of 4,696 tokens. Responses were about 800 tokens at p99.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Lowering &lt;code&gt;max_tokens&lt;/code&gt; to 1,024 cut the initial reservation to 1,624 tokens, about 65 percent smaller. The limit was still above p99. For unchanged input and output, final quota consumption stays the same. The smaller reservation leaves more room to admit requests. Monitor &lt;code&gt;stopReason&lt;/code&gt; to catch truncated responses. Use backoff and jitter, and cap retries.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Demand can also outrun the headroom you have on any one model. You want to know where work can go before you need it, and our evals answer that. The answer can be a model from another model family. For every major model we use, we keep at least one equivalent that performs as well on our evals and that we can load-balance to. Without those evals, choosing where the work goes would be guesswork.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If headroom on a model stays tight, we can rebalance by changing a default in our configuration. First check that the equivalent model has capacity for the extra load and that your quota covers it. Keep enough traffic on it to hold your access, too: after a model enters its Legacy period, existing customers can lose access after 15 days of inactivity. A Legacy model works until end-of-life, and after that Bedrock removes it from every Region, requests to it fail, and Bedrock doesn’t move your traffic automatically. Alarm when &lt;code&gt;LegacyModelInvocations&lt;/code&gt; rises above zero.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-6-whose-spend-is-it"&gt;Step 6: Whose spend is it?&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Bedrock runtime metrics are dimensioned by &lt;code&gt;ModelId&lt;/code&gt;, so you can see which model served a request but not which team sent it. Bedrock publishes these metrics both with and without the &lt;code&gt;ModelId&lt;/code&gt; dimension, and a request that fails before Bedrock resolves the model appears only in the series without it, so filtering by &lt;code&gt;ModelId&lt;/code&gt; undercounts errors. Bedrock offers three ways to attribute spend:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Which team or user? Bedrock records the caller’s IAM identity on every request. Tag your roles, or pass session tags, to see cost by tag in AWS Cost Explorer and the AWS Cost and Usage Report (CUR 2.0).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Which application? Application inference profiles are model-specific, so create one tagged profile per application and model, and call the matching profile’s ARN.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Which feature or prompt? Pass key-value pairs in &lt;code&gt;requestMetadata&lt;/code&gt; on &lt;code&gt;Converse&lt;/code&gt;. Bedrock writes them to the model invocation logs and leaves them off the bill. To price them, multiply the token counts in the logs by the published rates. Neither classic CUR nor CUR 2.0 carries a per-request ID, so reconcile the two by comparing totals by model, usage type and time period.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Behind a gateway, Bedrock sees the gateway’s role, so have the gateway assume a role for each user with its own &lt;code&gt;RoleSessionName&lt;/code&gt; or session tags. Cache those credentials for the session, because assuming a role on every request can exceed your AWS Security Token Service (AWS STS) rate quotas. Requests keep succeeding when attribution breaks, so set an alarm for when Bedrock fails to deliver invocation logs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Our usage records let us attribute usage by model, client, team and user, which we use only in aggregate for cost and capacity planning. We compute cost per task from the same four token counts, so we can compare token mix and cost between our evals and production.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We can go from a model release to a deployment decision overnight. After one rollout, average weekly spend per user on model A was several times that on the previous model. We moved its heavy users to a newer model. In their first full week, the group’s spend on its main model fell to about a seventh, with roughly the same number of requests. For every major model we use, our evals identify an equivalent we can rebalance to.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After testing, stop the eval jobs and agent processes you started. If you enabled model invocation logging only for this walkthrough, disable it in each Region using &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/APIReference/API_DeleteModelInvocationLoggingConfiguration.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;DeleteModelInvocationLoggingConfiguration&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After callers no longer use them, delete the application inference profiles you created using &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/APIReference/API_DeleteInferenceProfile.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;DeleteInferenceProfile&lt;/a&gt;. Remove IAM roles, policies, and resource tags created only for this walkthrough. Deactivate cost allocation tags used only for these tests. Delete test CloudWatch log groups and S3 log objects you no longer need.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We run four checks every time a model ships:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Release day: run the new model on your own tasks at every effort, and record a score and a cost per task together.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Before rollout: choose the default and cap effort from those numbers. Block any effort that costs more and scores less than a lower effort.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;First weeks: compare cost per request and average requests per user with the eval’s cost per task, pricing both from the same four token counts.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Before you need it: know which models score the same on your evals, from any model family, and check their capacity and your quota before you rebalance.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We chose Amazon Bedrock for access to multiple model families through one API. It lets us use our existing AWS access controls and logging while AWS manages the model infrastructure. We use evals to choose models for our work and observability to know when those choices need another look.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To go further, see these pages in the Amazon Bedrock User Guide:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/monitoring-runtime-metrics.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Monitor runtime metrics in CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/monitoring-runtime-otps.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Diagnose latency with output tokens per second&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/quotas-token-burndown.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;How tokens are counted&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-caching.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Prompt caching&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cost-management.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Track usage and costs&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-invocation-logging.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Monitor model invocations with logs&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-lifecycle.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Model lifecycle&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Design responsible AI controls for Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/design-responsible-ai-controls-for-amazon-aurora-postgresql/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[InduTeja Aligeti]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 18 Nov 2026 21:38:22 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Aurora]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">d20511fee78e1f57a74037658f9e257e5926dda8</guid>

					<description>Learn how to make RAG chatbot answers traceable with Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL, pgvector, and Amazon Bedrock Guardrails, logging sources, scores, and guardrail traces in SQL.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;A customer support chatbot that queries historical tickets and knowledge base articles stored in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/rds/aurora/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL-Compatible Edition&lt;/a&gt; can deliver fast, context-aware answers. However, it also introduces challenges around privacy, accuracy, safety, and governance, especially when the underlying data contains sensitive customer information. In this post, we map the eight AWS Responsible AI (RAI) dimensions to Aurora PostgreSQL capabilities. We then walk through a design pattern for the Explainability dimension, using a Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) based customer support chatbot as the reference use case. This post implements one dimension, Explainability, in depth. Treat it as Part 1 of a series rather than a full build-out of all eight.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/rds/aurora/postgresql-features/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL-Compatible Edition&lt;/a&gt; makes it more straightforward to operationalize responsible AI through its native &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonRDS/latest/AuroraUserGuide/aurora-ml.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Aurora Machine Learning (Aurora ML)&lt;/a&gt; capabilities. With Aurora ML, you can invoke &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker&lt;/a&gt; endpoints and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; foundation models directly from SQL, without data movement, custom extract, transform, and load (ETL) pipelines, or complex integrations. This post uses Aurora ML to generate vector embeddings from SQL, keeping the embedding pipeline inside the database layer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS defines eight core Responsible AI dimensions, published through the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/responsible-ai-lens/responsible-ai-lens.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Well-Architected Responsible AI Lens&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt;, and the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/responsible-ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Responsible Use of AI Guide&lt;/a&gt;. These dimensions (Fairness, Explainability, Privacy and Security, Safety, Controllability, Veracity and Robustness, Governance, and Transparency) provide a structured framework for evaluating and implementing trustworthy AI systems.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="use-case-overview"&gt;Use case overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Customer support systems often struggle to provide accurate, context-aware responses while maintaining privacy and compliance. Consider a customer support chatbot built on Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL that uses RAG with &lt;a href="https://github.com/pgvector/pgvector" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;pgvector&lt;/a&gt; embeddings, Amazon Bedrock model inference, and AWS Responsible AI guardrails to deliver grounded, explainable responses.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A customer can ask questions such as “Why was my last bill high?” or “Where is my last order?” and receive accurate answers with citations, while sensitive data remains obscured through personally identifiable information (PII) redaction and access controls.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="mapping-the-aws-responsible-ai-dimensions-to-aurora-postgresql"&gt;Mapping the AWS Responsible AI dimensions to Aurora PostgreSQL&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table maps the eight AWS Responsible AI dimensions to practical implementation strategies for a customer support chatbot built on Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL-Compatible Edition. It highlights how database features, AWS AI services, and governance controls work together to deliver a trustworthy, secure, and explainable RAG-based chatbot.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt;
