&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; These are example approaches and may vary by use case.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This reference architecture shows how Responsible AI controls map to each component of a RAG-based chatbot on Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL-Compatible Edition.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An ingestion pipeline redacts PII from support tickets, FAQs, and product documents, then converts them into vector embeddings. When a user submits a query, AWS Lambda runs a semantic search over Aurora PostgreSQL, applies Amazon Bedrock Guardrails for privacy and grounding, sends the guarded context to an Amazon Bedrock foundation model (FM), and logs every interaction for explainability and governance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Lambda grounds responses in enterprise data, redacts PII and applies Row-Level Security (RLS) for privacy, records citations and interaction logs for explainability, and writes audit records for governance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When to build this versus use a managed service: Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases provides built-in RAG with citations and is the simpler choice when default retrieval and citation behavior meets your needs. Build the custom logging design in this post when you need provenance stored in your own schema. That provenance includes retrieved versus cited sources, similarity scores, and guardrail traces that you can query with SQL and retain under your own policies.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram illustrates the end-to-end architecture of this solution, showing the data ingestion flow (left), the query and response flow (center), and the responsible AI controls applied at each stage (center and right).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/20463-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/20463-1.png" alt="Architecture diagram showing a user question flowing through API Gateway to Lambda inside a VPC. Lambda converts the question into a vector with Aurora ML and Titan Text Embeddings, runs a pgvector similarity search in Aurora PostgreSQL, sends the question and retrieved context to Amazon Bedrock for guardrail checks and grounded answer generation, and logs the interaction to Aurora. Supporting paths show audit export to Amazon S3 and logs to CloudWatch and CloudTrail." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Architecture of RAG-based customer support chatbot with Responsible AI controls on Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="data-ingestion"&gt;Data ingestion&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An ingestion pipeline loads historical support tickets, FAQs, and product documentation into Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL after redacting PII, using the &lt;code&gt;pgvector&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;aws_ml&lt;/code&gt; extensions for vector operations and Aurora ML integration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Aurora ML &lt;code&gt;aws_bedrock.invoke_model_get_embeddings&lt;/code&gt; function transforms the text into Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2 vectors (1,024 dimensions), and &lt;code&gt;pgvector&lt;/code&gt; stores them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Aurora PostgreSQL functions as both a relational store for structured customer data and a vector database for semantic search.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="query-and-response-flow"&gt;Query and response flow&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a user asks a question, the chatbot follows these steps (see Figure 1):&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The user sends a natural language question to Amazon API Gateway over HTTPS.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;API Gateway invokes AWS Lambda.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Turn the question into a vector. Lambda calls the Aurora ML &lt;code&gt;aws_bedrock.invoke_model_get_embeddings&lt;/code&gt; function with Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2. This must be the same model used to embed the knowledge base, so the question and documents both produce comparable 1,024-dimension vectors.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Run a similarity search. Aurora PostgreSQL uses the &lt;code&gt;pgvector&lt;/code&gt; cosine distance operator to compare the question vector with the stored knowledge vectors, and returns the most relevant chunks with their similarity scores. Row-Level Security limits results to the current tenant.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Build the augmented prompt. Lambda combines the user’s question with the retrieved chunks, labeling each one &lt;code&gt;[Source N]&lt;/code&gt;. This gives the model knowledge from your database that it would not otherwise have.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Generate a grounded answer. Lambda sends the prompt to Amazon Bedrock. Guardrails check the input (content filters, denied topics, PII), the foundation model writes an answer based on the retrieved chunks, and a contextual grounding check confirms the answer is supported by those sources.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Log the interaction for explainability. Lambda writes the complete interaction to the &lt;code&gt;chat_interactions&lt;/code&gt; table: the question, retrieved chunk IDs, similarity scores, the sources actually cited, the guardrail trace, and the model ID.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Lambda returns the answer, with its source citations, to the user.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Supporting operations&lt;/strong&gt; run separately from each request:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Ingestion:&lt;/strong&gt; document embeddings are created once, when content is loaded (see Data ingestion).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Retention:&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;pg_cron&lt;/code&gt; archives older interaction records to Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Monitoring:&lt;/strong&gt; Lambda sends logs and metrics to Amazon CloudWatch, and AWS CloudTrail records API activity for audit.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you begin, make sure you have the following in place:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL cluster with the &lt;code&gt;pgvector&lt;/code&gt; extension enabled. HNSW indexes and iterative index scans require newer &lt;code&gt;pgvector&lt;/code&gt; versions, and Aurora ML and Amazon Bedrock model support are versioned separately. See the Aurora ML, pgvector, and Bedrock documentation for the current minimum versions rather than pinning to a specific one.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The &lt;code&gt;aws_ml&lt;/code&gt; extension enabled on the cluster (&lt;code&gt;CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS aws_ml CASCADE&lt;/code&gt;), with an AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) role attached to the cluster granting &lt;code&gt;bedrock:InvokeModel&lt;/code&gt;. Aurora ML calls Bedrock in the cluster’s own Region.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The &lt;code&gt;aws_s3&lt;/code&gt; extension enabled on the cluster (&lt;code&gt;CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS aws_s3 CASCADE&lt;/code&gt;), with an IAM role attached for the &lt;code&gt;s3Export&lt;/code&gt; feature so the retention job can export to Amazon S3.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Bedrock access with Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2 (&lt;code&gt;amazon.titan-embed-text-v2:0&lt;/code&gt;) for embeddings and a foundation model that supports the Bedrock Converse API for answer generation. Model availability and recommended versions change over time, so choose a current model from Supported foundation models in Amazon Bedrock. Newer models are invoked through cross-Region inference profile IDs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails configured for PII protection, content filtering, and contextual grounding (enable the contextual grounding policy in your Guardrail configuration).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;IAM roles with appropriate permissions for Amazon Bedrock, Lambda, and Aurora PostgreSQL.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A custom cluster parameter group that adds &lt;code&gt;pgaudit&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;pg_cron&lt;/code&gt; to the existing &lt;code&gt;shared_preload_libraries&lt;/code&gt; value (append to it rather than replacing it, so defaults such as &lt;code&gt;pg_stat_statements&lt;/code&gt; are preserved. This requires a cluster restart). Set &lt;code&gt;cron.database_name&lt;/code&gt; to your target database.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Basic familiarity with PostgreSQL, SQL, and Python.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; The prerequisites listed here focus on the database layer. Your application layer (for example, AWS Lambda, Amazon API Gateway, and a chat UI) is separate and depends on your deployment architecture.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="design-patterns-for-the-explainability-dimension"&gt;Design patterns for the Explainability dimension&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This section presents the database-layer design patterns that make every AI-generated response traceable, from the user query, through retrieval and guardrail evaluation, to the final answer and its citations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-set-up-the-database-schema"&gt;Step 1: Set up the database schema&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create the core tables for customer data, chat sessions, interaction logging, and feedback:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Start by creating the schema and the tables you need. Each table serves a specific Responsible AI dimension:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;CREATE SCHEMA IF NOT EXISTS rai_postgresql;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The customers table stores profiles for tenant-aware access control:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;CREATE TABLE rai_postgresql.customers (
    customer_id  UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
    full_name    VARCHAR(150) NOT NULL,
    email        VARCHAR(255) UNIQUE NOT NULL,
    phone        VARCHAR(20),
    created_at   TIMESTAMP WITH TIME ZONE DEFAULT NOW(),
    is_active    BOOLEAN DEFAULT TRUE,
    metadata     JSONB DEFAULT '{}'
);&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The chat_interactions table is the core of the Explainability implementation. It logs every query-response cycle with full RAG provenance, including retrieved chunk IDs, similarity scores, cited versus retrieved sources, guardrail traces, and the model used:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;CREATE TABLE rai_postgresql.chat_interactions (
    interaction_id        UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
    session_id            UUID NOT NULL,
    customer_id           UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES rai_postgresql.customers(customer_id),
    turn_number           INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 1,
    request_text          TEXT NOT NULL,
    request_timestamp     TIMESTAMP WITH TIME ZONE DEFAULT NOW(),
    response_text         TEXT,
    response_timestamp    TIMESTAMP WITH TIME ZONE,
    response_latency_ms   INTEGER,
    intent                VARCHAR(100),
    confidence_score      NUMERIC(5, 4),
    -- Explainability columns
    retrieved_chunk_ids   UUID[],          -- IDs of chunks returned by pgvector
    similarity_scores     NUMERIC[],       -- cosine similarity per chunk
    citations             JSONB,           -- cited vs. all-retrieved sources
    guardrail_assessment  JSONB,           -- full Guardrail trace
    model_id              VARCHAR(100),    -- Bedrock model used
    guardrail_version     VARCHAR(20)      -- Guardrail version applied
);&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because &lt;code&gt;chat_interactions&lt;/code&gt; stores raw &lt;code&gt;request_text&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;response_text&lt;/code&gt;, protect it the same way as the source data: redact PII before the &lt;code&gt;INSERT&lt;/code&gt; (reusing the ingestion-time guardrail), or encrypt those columns with &lt;code&gt;pgcrypto&lt;/code&gt;, and apply Row-Level Security to the log tables so tenants cannot read each other’s interactions. Logging unprotected customer content would undercut the Privacy and Security control this design is meant to demonstrate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The chat_feedback table captures user ratings and comments on chatbot responses, which supports the Controllability dimension. The &lt;code&gt;ON DELETE CASCADE&lt;/code&gt; clause makes sure the 90-day retention purge does not fail with foreign key violations:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;CREATE TABLE rai_postgresql.chat_feedback (
    feedback_id     UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
    interaction_id  UUID NOT NULL
        REFERENCES rai_postgresql.chat_interactions(interaction_id)
        ON DELETE CASCADE,
    customer_id     UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES rai_postgresql.customers(customer_id),
    rating          SMALLINT NOT NULL CHECK (rating BETWEEN 1 AND 5),
    feedback_text   TEXT,
    created_at      TIMESTAMP WITH TIME ZONE DEFAULT NOW()
);&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-prepare-the-knowledge-base-with-pgvector-and-aurora-ml"&gt;Step 2: Prepare the knowledge base with pgvector and Aurora ML&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create the retrieval layer with vector embeddings, tenant isolation, and Aurora ML integration:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS vector;
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS aws_ml CASCADE;

CREATE TABLE rai_postgresql.knowledge_chunks (
    chunk_id    UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
    ticket_id   UUID NOT NULL,
    content     TEXT NOT NULL,
    embedding   VECTOR(1024),       -- Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2 (default 1024)
    metadata    JSONB,              -- source, date, category, product
    tenant_id   UUID,
    created_at  TIMESTAMP DEFAULT NOW()
);&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create an HNSW index for fast approximate nearest neighbor search:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;-- HNSW index for approximate nearest neighbor search
CREATE INDEX idx_chunks_embedding ON rai_postgresql.knowledge_chunks
USING hnsw (embedding vector_cosine_ops);&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Enforce tenant isolation with Row-Level Security so that vector search results are scoped to the current tenant before data leaves the database:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;-- Row-Level Security for tenant isolation (Privacy &amp;amp; Security)
-- FORCE RLS, non-owner role, safe current_setting
ALTER TABLE rai_postgresql.knowledge_chunks ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;
ALTER TABLE rai_postgresql.knowledge_chunks FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;

CREATE ROLE app_user;
GRANT USAGE ON SCHEMA rai_postgresql TO app_user;
GRANT SELECT ON rai_postgresql.knowledge_chunks TO app_user;

CREATE POLICY tenant_isolation ON rai_postgresql.knowledge_chunks
USING (
    current_setting('app.current_tenant', true) IS NOT NULL
    AND tenant_id = current_setting('app.current_tenant', true)::uuid
);&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Generate vector embeddings directly from SQL using Aurora ML, keeping the embedding pipeline inside the database layer:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;-- Generate embeddings using Aurora ML
-- No Lambda/boto3 needed for embeddings --- runs inside the database
UPDATE rai_postgresql.knowledge_chunks
SET embedding = aws_bedrock.invoke_model_get_embeddings(
    'amazon.titan-embed-text-v2:0',    -- model_id
    'application/json',                -- content_type
    'embedding',                       -- json_key to extract
    json_build_object('inputText', content)::text    -- model_input
)::vector
WHERE embedding IS NULL;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Always redact PII before inserting data into the knowledge base. Use Amazon Bedrock Guardrails (the &lt;code&gt;ApplyGuardrail&lt;/code&gt; API) or Amazon Comprehend (the &lt;code&gt;detect_pii_entities&lt;/code&gt; API) to identify and mask PII in your ingestion pipeline, as shown in the following example:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Example: Redact PII using Bedrock Guardrails ApplyGuardrail API
response = bedrock.apply_guardrail(guardrailIdentifier=guardrail_id,
    source='INPUT', content=[{'text': {'text': raw_text}}])
# outputs is empty when nothing is flagged; masking requires an ANONYMIZE PII policy (not BLOCK)
redacted_text = (response['outputs'][0]['text']
    if response.get('outputs') else raw_text)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-retrieve-context-and-generate-responses"&gt;Step 3: Retrieve context and generate responses&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a query reaches your application layer, retrieve the top matching knowledge chunks using the &lt;code&gt;pgvector&lt;/code&gt; cosine similarity operator. The following SQL query shows this pattern:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;-- Connect as app_user and set the tenant for this transaction so RLS returns rows
SET LOCAL app.current_tenant = '11111111-1111-1111-1111-111111111111';

-- Retrieve top 5 relevant chunks for a query
SELECT chunk_id, content, metadata,
    1 - (embedding &amp;lt;=&amp;gt; :query_embedding::vector) AS cosine_similarity
FROM rai_postgresql.knowledge_chunks
WHERE metadata -&amp;gt;&amp;gt; 'category' = :intent_category
    AND 1 - (embedding &amp;lt;=&amp;gt; :query_embedding::vector) &amp;gt;= 0.7 -- drop weak matches
ORDER BY embedding &amp;lt;=&amp;gt; :query_embedding::vector
LIMIT 5;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; The 0.7 cosine-similarity cutoff drops weakly related chunks so the model is not grounded on noise. Tune it against your own data. The category filter and this threshold both post-filter HNSW results and may return fewer than 5 rows. Raise &lt;code&gt;hnsw.ef_search&lt;/code&gt; or use iterative index scans (&lt;code&gt;pgvector&lt;/code&gt; 0.8.0+) to compensate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following pseudocode shows the key patterns for contextual grounding, guardrail-intervention handling, citation parsing, and interaction logging. The snippets focus on the Responsible AI logic rather than application plumbing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use &lt;code&gt;guardContent&lt;/code&gt; blocks to enable contextual grounding in the Bedrock Converse API:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Contextual grounding --- tag context and query for guardrail evaluation
response = bedrock.converse(
    modelId=model_id,
    messages=[{'role': 'user', 'content': [
        {'guardContent': {'text': {'text': context, 'qualifiers': ['grounding_source']}}},
        {'guardContent': {'text': {'text': query, 'qualifiers': ['query']}}},
        {'text': prompt}
    ]}],
    guardrailConfig={'guardrailIdentifier': guardrail_id,
        'guardrailVersion': '1', 'trace': 'enabled'},
    inferenceConfig={'maxTokens': 500, 'temperature': 0.1})&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Check &lt;code&gt;stopReason&lt;/code&gt; to handle cases where the guardrail blocks the response:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Handle guardrail intervention
if response.get('stopReason') == 'guardrail_intervened':
    answer = 'Unable to answer --- content flagged by guardrail.'
    trace = response.get('trace', {}).get('guardrail', {})
else:
    answer = response['output']['message']['content'][0]['text']&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Distinguish actually cited sources from all retrieved sources. On an Explainability post, this distinction is the point:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Parse [Source N] markers from the model answer
cited_indices = re.findall(r'\[Source (\d+)\]', answer)
cited = [retrieved[n-1] for n in map(int, cited_indices) if n-1 &amp;lt; len(retrieved)]
citations = {'cited_sources': cited, 'all_retrieved': retrieved,
    'citation_rate': len(cited) / len(retrieved) if retrieved else 0}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Log every interaction for Explainability. The &lt;code&gt;INSERT&lt;/code&gt; captures the full RAG provenance chain:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;INSERT INTO rai_postgresql.chat_interactions (
    session_id, customer_id, request_text, response_text,
    response_timestamp, response_latency_ms, intent, confidence_score,
    retrieved_chunk_ids, similarity_scores, citations,
    guardrail_assessment, model_id, guardrail_version
) VALUES (%s, %s, %s, %s, NOW(), %s, %s, %s, %s, %s, %s, %s, %s, %s);&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We define &lt;code&gt;confidence_score&lt;/code&gt; as the mean cosine similarity of the top-k retrieved chunks. The &lt;code&gt;intent&lt;/code&gt; field classifies the query (for example, &lt;code&gt;billing&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;order_status&lt;/code&gt;), and &lt;code&gt;response_latency_ms&lt;/code&gt; records end-to-end inference time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-log-interactions-and-add-governance"&gt;Step 4: Log interactions and add governance&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every chatbot interaction (the user query, retrieved chunks, similarity scores, response, and citations) is logged to the &lt;code&gt;chat_interactions&lt;/code&gt; table using the &lt;code&gt;INSERT&lt;/code&gt; shown in Step 3, supporting Explainability and Transparency. Customer feedback captured in &lt;code&gt;chat_feedback&lt;/code&gt; supports continuous improvement, reinforcing Controllability. For Governance, &lt;code&gt;pgaudit&lt;/code&gt; provides query audit trails, and &lt;code&gt;pg_cron&lt;/code&gt; drives automated data retention.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Set up &lt;code&gt;pgaudit&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;pg_cron&lt;/code&gt;, which both require &lt;code&gt;shared_preload_libraries&lt;/code&gt; (see Prerequisites):&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pgaudit;
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pg_cron;

-- Audit everything the application role does. In RDS/Aurora, object-level
-- auditing uses pgaudit.role = 'rds_pgaudit'; see the pgaudit docs for scope.
ALTER ROLE app_user SET pgaudit.log = 'write, ddl';
ALTER ROLE app_user SET pgaudit.log_relation = 'on';&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Query for governance review:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;-- Find low-confidence responses for review
SELECT interaction_id, request_text, response_text,
    confidence_score, intent, request_timestamp
FROM rai_postgresql.chat_interactions
WHERE confidence_score &amp;lt; 0.80
ORDER BY request_timestamp DESC LIMIT 10;

-- Analyze feedback by intent
SELECT ci.intent, AVG(cf.rating) AS avg_rating, COUNT(*) AS total,
    COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE cf.rating &amp;lt;= 2) AS low_ratings
FROM rai_postgresql.chat_feedback cf
JOIN rai_postgresql.chat_interactions ci
    ON cf.interaction_id = ci.interaction_id
GROUP BY ci.intent ORDER BY avg_rating ASC;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Archive both tables to Amazon S3 and purge only after the export succeeds. Running archive and purge as two independent cron jobs is unsafe: if the archive fails, a separately scheduled purge still deletes the rows. Wrap both in one function so the &lt;code&gt;DELETE&lt;/code&gt; runs only when the export returns. Also archive &lt;code&gt;chat_feedback&lt;/code&gt;, because the cascade would otherwise delete feedback that was not saved. The 90-day window matches a typical support-data retention policy. Adjust it to your own regulatory requirements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;-- One job: archive both tables, then purge only if the export succeeded
CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION rai_postgresql.archive_and_purge(cutoff INTERVAL DEFAULT '90 days')
RETURNS void LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
DECLARE exported bigint;
BEGIN
    -- Archive interactions due for purge
    SELECT rows_uploaded INTO exported FROM aws_s3.query_export_to_s3(
        format('SELECT * FROM rai_postgresql.chat_interactions
            WHERE request_timestamp &amp;lt; NOW() - %L', cutoff),
        aws_commons.create_s3_uri('your-audit-bucket',
            'chat-interactions/'||to_char(NOW(),'YYYY-MM-DD')||'.csv', 'us-east-1'));

    -- Archive the feedback that the cascade would otherwise delete
    PERFORM aws_s3.query_export_to_s3(
        format('SELECT cf.* FROM rai_postgresql.chat_feedback cf
            JOIN rai_postgresql.chat_interactions ci USING (interaction_id)
            WHERE ci.request_timestamp &amp;lt; NOW() - %L', cutoff),
        aws_commons.create_s3_uri('your-audit-bucket',
            'chat-feedback/'||to_char(NOW(),'YYYY-MM-DD')||'.csv', 'us-east-1'));

    -- Purge only after a successful export (feedback goes via ON DELETE CASCADE)
    IF exported IS NOT NULL THEN
        DELETE FROM rai_postgresql.chat_interactions
        WHERE request_timestamp &amp;lt; NOW() - cutoff;
    END IF;
END $$;

-- Schedule the single ordered job
SELECT cron.schedule('archive_and_purge', '0 0 * * *',
    $$SELECT rai_postgresql.archive_and_purge()$$);&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="validate-the-solution"&gt;Validate the solution&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Run one question end to end, then read the row it produced. That single row is the evidence the design works: it shows which chunks were retrieved, how similar they were, which sources the answer actually cited, and the guardrail outcome.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;SELECT request_text, model_id, confidence_score,
    retrieved_chunk_ids, similarity_scores,
    citations -&amp;gt; 'cited_sources' AS cited,
    guardrail_assessment -&amp;gt; 'action' AS guardrail_action
FROM rai_postgresql.chat_interactions
ORDER BY request_timestamp DESC LIMIT 1;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Expected: One row whose &lt;code&gt;cited_sources&lt;/code&gt; is a subset of &lt;code&gt;retrieved_chunk_ids&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;similarity_scores&lt;/code&gt; at or above your 0.7 threshold, and a &lt;code&gt;guardrail_action&lt;/code&gt; of &lt;code&gt;NONE&lt;/code&gt; for a clean question. If &lt;code&gt;cited_sources&lt;/code&gt; is empty, the model did not ground its answer and the response should be treated with caution.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="limitations-and-considerations"&gt;Limitations and considerations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails adds latency to each inference call and incurs per-text-unit charges. Contextual grounding has additional pricing. Benchmark your response times and costs before production deployment.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Interaction logging grows storage over time. The S3 archival and purge cron jobs help manage this, but monitor your Aurora storage and adjust the retention period as needed.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;HNSW indexes provide approximate nearest neighbor search. Post-filtering (for example, by category or tenant) runs after the index scan and may return fewer rows than your LIMIT. Increase &lt;code&gt;hnsw.ef_search&lt;/code&gt; or use iterative index scans (&lt;code&gt;pgvector&lt;/code&gt; 0.8.0+) to improve recall.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The contextual grounding check evaluates whether the model’s response is grounded in the provided context. It does not guarantee factual accuracy of the source documents themselves.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Aurora ML embedding generation runs synchronously in the database. For large batch ingestion, consider batching updates or using an asynchronous pipeline.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Everything in this post except Aurora ML (the &lt;code&gt;aws_bedrock.invoke_model_get_embeddings&lt;/code&gt; calls) works on Amazon Relational Database Service (Amazon RDS) for PostgreSQL as well.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete the following resources if you created them for this walkthrough:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Unschedule the cron job first, or it fails nightly once the schema is gone: &lt;code&gt;SELECT cron.unschedule('archive_and_purge');&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Drop the schema: &lt;code&gt;DROP SCHEMA rai_postgresql CASCADE;&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the Amazon Bedrock Guardrail.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Remove the AWS Lambda functions and API Gateway endpoints.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the Amazon S3 audit bucket (or configure a lifecycle policy).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the Aurora PostgreSQL cluster (or stop it if you plan to reuse it).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Remove IAM roles created for Bedrock and Aurora ML integration.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Drop the application role (roles are not removed by &lt;code&gt;DROP SCHEMA&lt;/code&gt;): &lt;code&gt;DROP ROLE app_user;&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cost considerations:&lt;/strong&gt; This solution uses Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL, Amazon Bedrock (model inference and embeddings), and Amazon Bedrock Guardrails. Costs vary by Region, instance size, request volume, and model choice. For pricing details, see the pricing pages for each service.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL-Compatible Edition, with &lt;code&gt;pgvector&lt;/code&gt;, Row-Level Security, &lt;code&gt;pgaudit&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;pgcrypto&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;pg_cron&lt;/code&gt;, and Aurora ML, provides database-layer building blocks for Responsible AI. Several dimensions also depend on AWS services outside the database, as the mapping table shows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we focused on the Explainability dimension as a detailed design walkthrough. By logging retrieved chunk IDs, similarity scores, cited sources, and guardrail traces in Aurora, you get a queryable audit trail at the database layer. The mapping table shows how the remaining dimensions build on the same foundation, combining Aurora PostgreSQL features with AWS AI services.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The key takeaways from these design patterns are:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Log retrieval provenance at query time: retrieved and cited chunk IDs, similarity scores, and the guardrail trace.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Distinguish the sources actually cited from everything retrieved. That distinction is what makes an answer explainable.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enforce tenant isolation with Row-Level Security, and set the tenant context before a vector search runs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Archive audit data before purging, and protect the log tables as carefully as the source data.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As a next step, explore &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases&lt;/a&gt;, the fully managed RAG capability that now supports Aurora PostgreSQL as a vector store in many AWS Regions and provides built-in citation generation, which can help reduce the custom integration code required for Explainability in new projects.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Private certificate authority support in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/private-certificate-authority-support-in-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-identity/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Aastha Paul]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 18 Nov 2026 20:53:41 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">8ef977d6239789a84140a268afc45f39deb45964</guid>

					<description>Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Identity now supports private certificate authorities (CAs), so your agents can connect to self-hosted identity providers that use a private CA. This post shows how to issue an IdP certificate with AWS Private CA, configure a credential provider to trust it, rotate the CA, and protect the certificate.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Many teams that build applications self-host their identity provider (IdP), such as Keycloak or PingFederate, inside a private network. These IdPs usually present a TLS server certificate from a private certificate authority (CA). With private CA support in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Identity&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, your agents can now connect to these IdPs while trusting the private CA that your organization already uses.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore Identity now supports private CA certificates for private identity providers. You store the PEM-encoded CA certificate in AWS Secrets Manager or Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity-outbound-credential-provider.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;reference it from your credential provider&lt;/a&gt;. The CA certificate contains only the public certificate, not its private key. AgentCore Identity uses it to validate the server certificate presented by your IdP’s discovery, JSON Web Key Set (JWKS), and token endpoints. The CA certificate must be a root or intermediate CA certificate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we show you how to issue an IdP certificate with AWS Private Certificate Authority. We then configure an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity-outbound-credential-provider.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Identity credential provider&lt;/a&gt; to trust it and rotate the CA while minimizing downtime. We also show how to protect the CA certificate so that only your IdP team can change it. Although AgentCore Identity supports any private CA, including enterprise, third-party, and self-signed root CAs, the walkthrough in this post uses AWS Private CA and Keycloak as the IdP.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Consider an agent that needs to call an internal wiki API protected by your Keycloak IdP. The agent runs on &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agents-tools-runtime.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Runtime&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, which hosts your agent code. To get an OAuth 2.0 access token from Keycloak, the agent uses an AgentCore Identity credential provider, which holds the IdP connection details and client credentials.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows this setup. Keycloak runs in your virtual private cloud (VPC) behind an internal Application Load Balancer. The load balancer presents an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/certificate-manager/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Certificate Manager (ACM)&lt;/a&gt; private certificate signed by AWS Private CA. The CA certificate that AgentCore Identity trusts is stored in Secrets Manager or Amazon S3.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore Identity reaches the IdP privately through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/vpc/lattice/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon VPC Lattice&lt;/a&gt;, which connects to your VPC through a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/vpc-lattice/latest/ug/resource-gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;resource gateway&lt;/a&gt;, an entry point for VPC Lattice traffic into your VPC. VPC Lattice is required for private CA support. If you choose the managed option, AgentCore Identity creates and manages the VPC Lattice resources for you.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22407-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22407-1.png" alt="AgentCore Identity reaches a Keycloak IdP through VPC Lattice and validates its server certificate against a private CA." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Connecting AgentCore Identity to a self-hosted IdP that uses a private CA&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The flow works as follows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;You store the CA certificate in Secrets Manager or Amazon S3.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;You create a credential provider that points to your IdP and to the CA certificate.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AgentCore Identity reads the CA certificate and checks that it’s a valid CA certificate.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Your agent requests an access token from AgentCore Identity.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AgentCore Identity connects to the IdP through VPC Lattice and checks the load balancer certificate against your CA.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The IdP issues the token and AgentCore Identity returns it to your agent.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agents that use machine-to-machine, on-behalf-of, and user-delegated flows can also use the private CA without changing their code.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To follow this walkthrough, you need:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/resources/create-account/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS account&lt;/a&gt; with access to the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore console&lt;/a&gt;, in a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agentcore-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;supported AWS Region&lt;/a&gt; where AgentCore Identity supports private IdPs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A self-hosted, OpenID Connect (OIDC) compliant IdP that runs in your VPC, with an OAuth client registered for your agent. The networking requirements, including subnets, security groups, and a private hosted zone for the IdP hostname, are described in &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity-private-idp.html#private-idp-prerequisites" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Connect to private identity providers&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The latest version of the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/userguide/getting-started-install.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI)&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://openssl-library.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenSSL&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Permission to create credential providers, plus read access to the CA certificate. AgentCore Identity reads the certificate with the credentials of the principal that calls &lt;code&gt;CreateOauth2CredentialProvider&lt;/code&gt;, so that principal needs &lt;code&gt;secretsmanager:GetSecretValue&lt;/code&gt; on the secret or &lt;code&gt;s3:GetObject&lt;/code&gt; on the object. If the secret or object is encrypted with a customer managed AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS) key, the principal also needs &lt;code&gt;kms:Decrypt&lt;/code&gt; on that key.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="considerations"&gt;Considerations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Keep the following in mind when you plan your deployment:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Private IdPs only&lt;/strong&gt;: A credential provider or authorizer must have a &lt;code&gt;privateEndpoint&lt;/code&gt; to use a private CA. Otherwise, the request fails with the message &lt;code&gt;The private certificate authority feature can only be used with private IdP&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;CA certificates in PEM format&lt;/strong&gt;: The file must be PEM-encoded and no larger than 16 KB. It must contain only root or intermediate CA certificates that are within their validity period.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;One certificate source per provider&lt;/strong&gt;: Each credential provider references one secret or one S3 object. To trust more than one CA, put the certificates in the same PEM file.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Storage location&lt;/strong&gt;: The secret or S3 object must be in the same AWS Region as the credential provider. Secrets Manager secrets must also be in the same account as the credential provider. S3 objects can be in another account when you provide &lt;code&gt;bucketOwnerAccountId&lt;/code&gt; and the bucket policy grants access.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Regions&lt;/strong&gt;: Private CA support depends on VPC Lattice and is available in the AWS Regions where AgentCore Identity supports private IdPs.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="walkthrough"&gt;Walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The walkthrough has four steps. If your IdP already presents a certificate from your enterprise CA, skip to Step 2.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-issue-a-server-certificate-for-your-idp"&gt;Step 1: Issue a server certificate for your IdP&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Start by creating a root CA in AWS Private CA. For instructions, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/privateca/latest/userguide/create-CA.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Create a private CA&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/privateca/latest/userguide/PCACertInstall.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Install the CA certificate&lt;/a&gt;. Choose general-purpose mode, because short-lived certificate mode issues certificates that are valid for 7 days or less. When you create the CA, authorize ACM to renew certificates, so that ACM can renew the IdP’s server certificate before it expires.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Next, request the server certificate. AgentCore Identity checks that the certificate matches the IdP hostname, so include each hostname that appears in your IdP’s discovery document as a subject alternative name (SAN). In most Keycloak deployments, the discovery, JWKS, and token endpoints share one hostname. For example, if your discovery URL is &lt;code&gt;https://keycloak.example.local/realms/agents/.well-known/openid-configuration&lt;/code&gt;, add &lt;code&gt;keycloak.example.local&lt;/code&gt; as a SAN.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To request a private certificate for the IdP (console)&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/acm/home" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;ACM console&lt;/a&gt; and choose Request.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose Request a private certificate and then choose Next.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For Certificate authority, choose the CA that you created.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For Fully qualified domain name, enter the IdP hostname, for example &lt;code&gt;keycloak.example.local&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose Request.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Attach the certificate to the HTTPS listener of the internal load balancer in front of your IdP. For instructions, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/elasticloadbalancing/latest/application/listener-update-certificates.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Update an HTTPS listener&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-store-the-ca-certificate"&gt;Step 2: Store the CA certificate&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore Identity needs to trust the CA certificate to validate the IdP’s server certificate. Because it trusts the CA rather than the server certificate itself, you don’t need to update the credential provider each time the server certificate renews.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Export the CA certificate from AWS Private CA in PEM format:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws acm-pca get-certificate-authority-certificate \
  --certificate-authority-arn arn:aws:acm-pca:us-east-1:111122223333:certificate-authority/11223344-1234-1122-2233-112233445566 \
  --query Certificate \
  --output text &amp;gt; idp-root-ca.pem&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you upload the file, check it &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity-private-idp.html#identity-private-certificate-requirements" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;against the requirements&lt;/a&gt;. The file must be PEM-encoded and meet size requirements, containing only CA certificates that are within their validity period. The following commands show the CA flag, expiry date, and file size. The last command confirms that the IdP’s certificate chains to this CA. Run it from a host that can reach the IdP. It returns Verify return code: 0 (ok) when the chain is valid.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;openssl x509 -in idp-root-ca.pem -noout -subject -enddate
openssl x509 -in idp-root-ca.pem -noout -text | grep "CA:TRUE"
wc -c &amp;lt; idp-root-ca.pem
openssl s_client -connect keycloak.example.local:443 -servername keycloak.example.local \
  -CAfile idp-root-ca.pem &amp;lt;/dev/null 2&amp;gt;/dev/null | grep "Verify return code"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The output looks similar to the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;subject=CN=Example Corp Root CA
notAfter=Nov 10 00:00:00 2035 GMT
                CA:TRUE
    1489
Verify return code: 0 (ok)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Store the file in Secrets Manager or Amazon S3. A Secrets Manager secret must be a string secret in the same account as the credential provider. An S3 object can be in another account, which suits organizations that run the IdP in a central account.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;# Option A: AWS Secrets Manager
aws secretsmanager create-secret \
  --name agentcore/keycloak-root-ca \
  --secret-string file://idp-root-ca.pem
# Option B: Amazon S3
aws s3 cp idp-root-ca.pem s3://amzn-s3-demo-bucket/trust-anchors/keycloak-root-ca.pem&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Give write access to the secret or object only to the team that operates your IdP. For an example policy, see Protect the CA certificate later in this post.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-create-the-credential-provider"&gt;Step 3: Create the credential provider&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can create the credential provider in the AgentCore console or with the AWS CLI. The private CA settings appear on the console only after you connect the OAuth client to a VPC, because private CA support applies only to private IdPs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To create a credential provider that trusts your private CA (console)&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Open the AgentCore console&lt;/a&gt; and in the navigation pane under Connections, choose &lt;strong&gt;Outbound Auth&lt;/strong&gt;. Then choose &lt;strong&gt;Add OAuth client&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For &lt;strong&gt;Provider&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Customized provider&lt;/strong&gt;. For &lt;strong&gt;Configuration type&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Discovery URL&lt;/strong&gt; and enter your IdP’s discovery URL, for example &lt;code&gt;https://keycloak.example.local/realms/agents/.well-known/openid-configuration&lt;/code&gt;. Then enter the client ID and client secret.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Expand Additional configurations. Under &lt;strong&gt;VPC security&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Managed&lt;/strong&gt; and select the VPC, subnets, and security groups that can reach the IdP. If you manage your own VPC Lattice resources, choose &lt;strong&gt;Self-managed&lt;/strong&gt; and select the resource configuration instead.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Under &lt;strong&gt;Root certificate authority&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Add root certificate&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the &lt;strong&gt;Add root certificate&lt;/strong&gt; panel, for &lt;strong&gt;Certificate source&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;AWS Secrets Manager&lt;/strong&gt; and select the secret that you created in Step 2. To use Amazon S3, choose &lt;strong&gt;Amazon S3&lt;/strong&gt; and enter the S3 URI. For an object in another account, also enter the Bucket owner account ID.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Apply&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Add OAuth client&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Root certificate authority field accepts either a root or an intermediate CA certificate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22407-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22407-2.png" alt="The AgentCore console Add root certificate panel with AWS Secrets Manager selected as the certificate source." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Adding a root certificate from AWS Secrets Manager&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To create the same credential provider with the AWS CLI, add certificateConfigurations to the custom OAuth 2.0 provider configuration:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws bedrock-agentcore-control create-oauth2-credential-provider \
  --name keycloak-private-ca \
  --credential-provider-vendor CustomOauth2 \
  --oauth2-provider-config-input '{
    "customOauth2ProviderConfig": {
      "oauthDiscovery": {
        "discoveryUrl": "https://keycloak.example.local/realms/agents/.well-known/openid-configuration"
      },
      "clientId": "agentcore-client",
      "clientSecret": "&amp;lt;client-secret&amp;gt;",
      "privateEndpoint": {
        "managedVpcResource": {
          "vpcIdentifier": "vpc-0abc123def456",
          "subnetIds": ["subnet-0abc123", "subnet-0def456"],
          "securityGroupIds": ["sg-0abc123def456"],
          "endpointIpAddressType": "IPV4"
        }
      },
      "certificateConfigurations": [
        {
          "secretsManager": {
            "secretArn": "arn:aws:secretsmanager:us-east-1:111122223333:secret:agentcore/keycloak-root-ca-AbCdEf"
          }
        }
      ]
    }
  }'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To use Amazon S3 instead, replace the &lt;code&gt;certificateConfigurations&lt;/code&gt; entry with &lt;code&gt;{"s3": {"uri": "s3://amzn-s3-demo-bucket/trust-anchors/keycloak-root-ca.pem", "bucketOwnerAccountId": "444455556666"}}&lt;/code&gt;. If you leave out bucketOwnerAccountId, AgentCore Identity expects the bucket to be in your own account. To keep the client secret out of your shell history, you can &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/reference-your-own-aws-secrets-manager-secrets-in-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-identity/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;reference a client secret that you store in Secrets Manager&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-verify-the-credential-provider"&gt;Step 4: Verify the credential provider&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore Identity validates the certificate and sets up private connectivity in the background, so a new credential provider starts in the &lt;code&gt;CREATING&lt;/code&gt; status. Check the status after a few minutes:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws bedrock-agentcore-control get-oauth2-credential-provider \
  --name keycloak-private-ca \
  --query '{status: status, failureReason: failureReason}'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On the console, the credential provider’s details page shows the configured certificate under &lt;strong&gt;Root certificates&lt;/strong&gt;. When the status is READY, your agents can request tokens from the IdP. If the status is &lt;code&gt;CREATE_FAILED&lt;/code&gt;, the &lt;code&gt;failureReason&lt;/code&gt; field explains why. For example, &lt;code&gt;Leaf certificates are not allowed, must provide CA certificates only&lt;/code&gt; means that you uploaded the server certificate instead of the CA certificate. If you see &lt;code&gt;Unable to access S3 object&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;Secrets Manager can't find the specified secret&lt;/code&gt;, check that the URI or Amazon Resource Name (ARN) is correct. Also confirm that your identity can read the object or secret. If the provider is &lt;code&gt;READY&lt;/code&gt; but token requests fail, confirm that the IdP’s server certificate was issued by the configured CA and has a SAN that matches the IdP hostname.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="monitor-and-rotate-the-ca-certificate"&gt;Monitor and rotate the CA certificate&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After you verify that the credential provider trusts the private CA, monitor the CA certificate’s expiration and rotate it before it expires to prevent token request failures. AgentCore Identity publishes the &lt;code&gt;EarliestCertificateDaysToExpiry&lt;/code&gt; metric to Amazon CloudWatch in the &lt;code&gt;AWS/Bedrock-AgentCore&lt;/code&gt; namespace when your agents request tokens through a credential provider that uses a private CA. The metric reports the days remaining until the earliest certificate in your PEM file expires. It has &lt;code&gt;ProviderName&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ProviderArn&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;TokenVault&lt;/code&gt; dimensions. When you create an alarm, specify all three. We recommend a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonCloudWatch/latest/monitoring/AlarmThatSendsEmail.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CloudWatch alarm&lt;/a&gt; that notifies you well before the value reaches zero, because token requests to the IdP stop working when the CA certificate expires.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;NOTE&lt;/strong&gt;: AgentCore Identity reads the certificate only when you create or update the credential provider. If you change the secret or S3 object, call &lt;code&gt;UpdateOauth2CredentialProvider&lt;/code&gt; afterward so that AgentCore Identity reads the new content. Include &lt;code&gt;certificateConfigurations&lt;/code&gt; in the update request. If you leave it out, the credential provider stops trusting your private CA.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A PEM file can contain more than one CA certificate and AgentCore Identity trusts each of them. You can use this to rotate to a new CA while minimizing downtime:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Combine the current and new CA certificates in one file, for example with &lt;code&gt;cat current-ca.pem new-ca.pem &amp;gt; rotation.pem&lt;/code&gt;. Upload the file to your secret or S3 object.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Call &lt;code&gt;UpdateOauth2CredentialProvider&lt;/code&gt; and wait for the status to return to &lt;code&gt;READY&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Replace the IdP’s server certificate with one issued by the new CA.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;After token requests succeed, upload a file that contains only the new CA certificate and call &lt;code&gt;UpdateOauth2CredentialProvider&lt;/code&gt; again.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore Identity doesn’t check certificate revocation lists (CRLs) or use the Online Certificate Status Protocol (OCSP). This means that if an unauthorized party obtains the private key for an IdP server certificate, AgentCore Identity continues to trust that certificate. This trust lasts until the certificate expires or until you stop trusting the CA that issued it. To stop trusting a CA, remove it from the PEM file and update the credential provider.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="protect-the-ca-certificate"&gt;Protect the CA certificate&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A CA certificate contains no private key and is public by design, because you hand it to every client that has to trust your IdP. What you need to protect is the data integrity of the trust store, meaning the secret or S3 object that holds the certificate. If someone replaces the certificate or adds another CA certificate to the trust store, the next update could let an unauthorized party who can redirect traffic impersonate your IdP.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We recommend that you scope permissions around writes rather than reads. Give write access, such as &lt;code&gt;s3:PutObject&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;secretsmanager:PutSecretValue&lt;/code&gt;, only to the team or pipeline that operates the IdP. Give read access to the principals that call &lt;code&gt;CreateOauth2CredentialProvider&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;UpdateOauth2CredentialProvider&lt;/code&gt;. During these operations, AgentCore Identity reads the CA certificate using the caller’s IAM permissions. The following bucket policy for a central identity account (444455556666) lets only the role that manages credential providers in a workload account (111122223333) read the CA certificates. It also requires that role to belong to your organization in AWS Organizations, and it denies writes from principals other than the IdP administrator role:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "AllowCredentialProviderAdminRead",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Principal": { "AWS": "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/AgentCoreCredentialProviderAdmin" },
      "Action": "s3:GetObject",
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::amzn-s3-demo-bucket/trust-anchors/*",
      "Condition": {
        "StringEquals": { "aws:PrincipalOrgID": "o-exampleorgid" }
      }
    },
    {
      "Sid": "DenyWritesExceptIdpAdmin",
      "Effect": "Deny",
      "Principal": "*",
      "Action": ["s3:PutObject", "s3:DeleteObject"],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::amzn-s3-demo-bucket/trust-anchors/*",
      "Condition": {
        "ArnNotLike": { "aws:PrincipalArn": "arn:aws:iam::444455556666:role/IdpAdmin" }
      }
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The principal in the workload account also needs &lt;code&gt;s3:GetObject&lt;/code&gt; in its own IAM policy. For Secrets Manager, which must be in the same account, use a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/secretsmanager/latest/userguide/auth-and-access_resource-policies.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;resource-based policy&lt;/a&gt; on the secret to restrict writes to the IdP administrator role.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because the secret is in the same account, IAM policies can grant read access, so the resource policy only needs to deny changes:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "DenyChangesExceptIdpAdmin",
      "Effect": "Deny",
      "Principal": "*",
      "Action": [
        "secretsmanager:PutSecretValue",
        "secretsmanager:UpdateSecret",
        "secretsmanager:UpdateSecretVersionStage",
        "secretsmanager:RotateSecret",
        "secretsmanager:DeleteSecret",
        "secretsmanager:RestoreSecret",
        "secretsmanager:PutResourcePolicy",
        "secretsmanager:DeleteResourcePolicy"
      ],
      "Resource": "*",
      "Condition": {
        "ArnNotLike": { "aws:PrincipalArn": "arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/IdpAdmin" }
      }
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Turn on &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/Versioning.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;S3 Versioning&lt;/a&gt; to recover an overwritten file and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/awscloudtrail/latest/userguide/logging-data-events-with-cloudtrail.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CloudTrail data events&lt;/a&gt; to record each change. Because the CA certificate isn’t confidential, SSE-S3 encryption is sufficient and avoids the cross-account AWS KMS permissions that a customer managed key requires.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Keep the trust scope of each credential provider narrow. Upload only the CA that issues your IdP’s server certificate, rather than your organization’s full trust bundle. If your IdPs use different CAs, store each CA certificate in its own secret or S3 object so that each credential provider trusts only the CA that it needs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="use-a-private-ca-for-inbound-authorization"&gt;Use a private CA for inbound authorization&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can also use a private CA for inbound authorization. When AgentCore Runtime or AgentCore Gateway, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, validates JSON Web Tokens (JWTs) issued by a private IdP, it fetches the IdP’s discovery document and its signing keys from the JWKS endpoint. AgentCore Gateway validates the discovery URL when you create the gateway. If the CA certificate is missing or doesn’t match the IdP’s certificate chain, the gateway status changes to &lt;code&gt;FAILED&lt;/code&gt; with the reason &lt;code&gt;Failed to validate discovery url&lt;/code&gt;. To trust a private CA for that connection, add &lt;code&gt;certificateConfigurations&lt;/code&gt; to the &lt;code&gt;customJWTAuthorizer&lt;/code&gt; configuration, next to the existing &lt;code&gt;privateEndpoint&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;"customJWTAuthorizer": {
  "discoveryUrl": "https://keycloak.example.local/realms/agents/.well-known/openid-configuration",
  "allowedClients": ["agentcore-client"],
  "privateEndpoint": {
    "managedVpcResource": {
      "vpcIdentifier": "vpc-0abc123def456",
      "subnetIds": ["subnet-0abc123", "subnet-0def456"],
      "securityGroupIds": ["sg-0abc123def456"],
      "endpointIpAddressType": "IPV4"
    }
  },
  "certificateConfigurations": [
    { "s3": { "uri": "s3://amzn-s3-demo-bucket/trust-anchors/keycloak-root-ca.pem", "bucketOwnerAccountId": "444455556666" } }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete the resources that you created:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the credential provider in the AgentCore console, or run &lt;code&gt;aws bedrock-agentcore-control delete-oauth2-credential-provider --name keycloak-private-ca&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the secret in Secrets Manager or the object in Amazon S3.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Remove the certificate from the load balancer listener and then delete it in ACM.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Disable and then delete the private CA in the AWS Private CA console. AWS Private CA charges a monthly fee for each CA until you delete it. For details, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/private-ca/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Private CA pricing&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed how to connect AgentCore Identity to a self-hosted IdP that uses a certificate from your own CA. You issued a server certificate with AWS Private CA, stored the CA certificate in Secrets Manager or Amazon S3 and referenced it from a credential provider. You also saw how to rotate the CA with a combined PEM file and how to limit who can change the certificate that your agents trust.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity-private-idp.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Connect to private identity providers in the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Developer Guide&lt;/a&gt;. For examples using Keycloak, see the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/05-authenticate-and-authorize/01-inbound-auth/05-inbound-auth-keycloak" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore samples repository on GitHub&lt;/a&gt;. If you have questions or feedback, leave a comment on this post.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;
