			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Connect Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway to private APIs in a hub-and-spoke network with centralized inspection</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/connect-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-gateway-to-private-apis-in-a-hub-and-spoke-network-with-centralized-inspection/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Puneeth Komaragiri]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 18 Nov 2026 19:05:45 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">193eac1997a095a08f8e818e6f6e83666eaf07b1</guid>

					<description>Many enterprises run a centralized network model: one account owns every VPC and a central firewall inspects all traffic. This post shows a tested pattern for connecting Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway to private APIs and MCP servers in that model, with the VPC Lattice resource gateway in the network account so every hop crosses the firewall.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;In many enterprises, a central network team owns every virtual private cloud (VPC) and subnet, and every flow between VPCs must pass a central firewall in both directions. Agents that call internal REST APIs and Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers through &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, must meet the same two requirements. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore is a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post shows a pattern that meets both requirements. The network team keeps ownership of every subnet, and traffic between the Gateway and your private APIs and MCP servers crosses the central firewall in both directions. It covers where to place the Amazon VPC Lattice resource gateway that the Gateway uses to reach private targets. It also covers who owns each piece, six configuration decisions, and the checks that confirm the pattern works.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We built and tested the pattern in the US East (N. Virginia) AWS Region (&lt;code&gt;us-east-1&lt;/code&gt;), in a lab with three member accounts and a management account in AWS Organizations. The AWS CloudFormation templates, test scripts, test evidence, and reference material (address plan, full validation checks, troubleshooting, quotas) are in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-agentcore-gateway-hub-spoke-inspection" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;companion repository&lt;/a&gt;. Use the pattern as a guideline and adjust it to your own account architecture.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="terms-used-in-this-post"&gt;Terms used in this post&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Resource gateway:&lt;/strong&gt; The &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/vpc-lattice/latest/ug/what-is-vpc-lattice.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon VPC Lattice&lt;/a&gt; entry point into a VPC: elastic network interfaces (ENIs) in subnets you choose. Backends see its addresses as the source of each connection.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Resource configuration:&lt;/strong&gt; A VPC Lattice object that names one backend (here, an internal load balancer or VPC endpoint DNS name and port) behind a resource gateway. You can share it with other accounts through AWS Resource Access Manager (AWS RAM).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Managed mode and self-managed mode:&lt;/strong&gt; The two &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/vpc-egress-private-endpoints.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;private endpoint&lt;/a&gt; options for Gateway targets. In managed mode, AgentCore creates the resource gateway for you. In self-managed mode, you create it and give the target a resource configuration.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="when-to-use-this-pattern"&gt;When to use this pattern&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use this pattern when all of the following are true:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A network account owns every VPC, subnet, and AWS Transit Gateway attachment, and shares application subnets through AWS RAM.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A central firewall in an inspection VPC must inspect traffic entering and leaving every VPC.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Application teams manage only their own resources and security groups.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Otherwise, one of the options in Table 1 fits better.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Table 1: When another option fits better.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Your environment&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The Gateway account can own subnets that reach your tools&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/vpc-egress-private-endpoints.html#lattice-vpc-egress-managed-lattice" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Managed mode&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Traffic between the Gateway and the tools doesn’t need central inspection&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/vpc-egress-private-endpoints.html#lattice-vpc-egress-self-managed-lattice" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Self-managed mode&lt;/a&gt;, as documented&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Tools are reachable on public endpoints&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A standard &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Gateway target&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We also considered managed mode in subnets that AgentCore owns, a resource gateway in each tool VPC or in the central endpoints VPC, and internet-facing load balancers with AWS WAF per tool. The &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-agentcore-gateway-hub-spoke-inspection/blob/main/docs/considerations.md#alternatives-we-considered" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;alternatives write-up&lt;/a&gt; explains why each falls short under these constraints.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="design-requirements-for-centralized-inspection"&gt;Design requirements for centralized inspection&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The network account owns every subnet. The Gateway and application accounts build only in subnets shared to them through AWS RAM.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Every flow between VPCs crosses the central firewall, in both directions. The central firewall in the inspection VPC inspects the traffic between the Gateway and your private APIs and MCP servers.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore Gateway reaches private targets through a VPC Lattice resource gateway, and the two private endpoint modes place it differently. Managed mode creates it in the Gateway account, which fits environments where that account can own the subnets it runs in. Self-managed mode, as documented, places it in subnets with direct access to the backend, which fits environments that do not require inspection between the Gateway and the tool. This environment needs both conditions at once, so the pattern uses self-managed mode with one change. The network account owns the resource gateway and places it in subnets routed through AWS Transit Gateway, so every request and reply passes the inspection VPC.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Give the resource gateway its own VPC and subnets in the network account. Don’t share them, and keep backends out of them. Route them through the transit gateway so every request and reply between VPCs crosses the firewall. Share one resource configuration per backend with the AgentCore account through AWS RAM. Figure 1 shows the accounts, VPCs, and traffic path.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/22132-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/22132-1.png" alt="Network account owns every VPC and the VPC Lattice resource gateway; traffic crosses the central firewall in both directions" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: The network account owns every VPC and the resource gateway. The other accounts own only what they place in shared subnets. Solid lines cross the firewall in both directions. Step numbers match the following traffic path&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="traffic-path"&gt;Traffic path&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The agent, in a shared subnet with no internet route, calls the Gateway URL. Private DNS on the Gateway interface endpoint resolves it inside the agent’s VPC.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS WAF, if attached, evaluates the request. The Gateway checks its resource policy and the inbound JSON Web Token (JWT), then picks the target and tool.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The Gateway gets an outbound OAuth token for the tool account and sends the request through VPC Lattice to the resource gateway in the network account.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The resource gateway subnets route to the transit gateway, which sends the traffic through the inspection VPC to the tool account’s internal Application Load Balancer (ALB) or &lt;code&gt;execute-api&lt;/code&gt; endpoint. (Crosses the firewall.)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The ALB or Amazon API Gateway validates the token and forwards the request to the backend.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The response returns the same way. (Crosses the firewall.)&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;How does Gateway traffic enter the network account’s VPC without a transit gateway attachment in the AgentCore account? When you create a target, AgentCore associates the shared resource configuration with its own VPC Lattice service network. VPC Lattice then delivers connections from that service network to the resource gateway’s network interfaces in your VPC. AWS operates that hop, and from there your routes and firewall take over.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;What this pattern doesn’t do. Check these limits against your requirements before you adopt it:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No payload inspection –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Traffic is TLS end to end from the Gateway to the ALB, with a certificate the Gateway validates. The firewall sees flow metadata and the TLS Server Name Indication (SNI), not payloads. Request content is in the Gateway, AWS WAF, and ALB logs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Some legs skip the firewall –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;The agent-to-interface-endpoint leg and the Gateway-to-resource-gateway leg don’t cross it. Neither do the token requests that AgentCore makes to each identity provider.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No end-user identity at the tool –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;With the client credentials grant, every call that reaches the tool carries the Gateway’s client identity. To trace a call to a caller, use the inbound JWT claims in the Gateway application logs.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="who-owns-what"&gt;Who owns what&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Table 2: Who creates what.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;One time&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;New API on a connected ALB&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;New ALB or tool account&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Network team&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Resource gateway and its VPC, subnets, and routes. Firewall rules. A default route from the tool VPC to the transit gateway for the ALB’s signing-key fetch. Gateway interface endpoint and policy. AWS RAM share&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Nothing&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;One resource configuration added to the share. A firewall rule if the range is new&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Tool team&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;An ALB certificate whose subject alternative name matches the target host name that the AgentCore team uses&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A listener rule (with &lt;code&gt;jwt-validation&lt;/code&gt;) on their ALB&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;An internal ALB and security group, and an app client for the Gateway (client credentials grant, with a scope such as &lt;code&gt;tools/invoke&lt;/code&gt;)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AgentCore team&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Gateway, inbound authorizer, resource policy, optional AWS WAF&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;One Gateway target per API or MCP server&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A target, plus an OAuth credential provider for a new identity provider&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A new operation or tool on an existing target needs no new target: update the OpenAPI spec with &lt;code&gt;UpdateGatewayTarget&lt;/code&gt;, or call &lt;code&gt;SynchronizeGatewayTargets&lt;/code&gt; for an MCP server. The repository’s &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-agentcore-gateway-hub-spoke-inspection/blob/main/docs/build-order.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;build order&lt;/a&gt; lists the steps in each account and the values the teams pass to each other.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An organization in AWS Organizations with resource sharing in AWS RAM enabled.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI) 2.37.7 or later, or the AWS SDK for Python (Boto3) 1.43.106 or later, for the &lt;code&gt;certificateConfigurations&lt;/code&gt; parameter.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For the role that creates Gateway targets: &lt;code&gt;vpc-lattice:GetResourceConfiguration&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;vpc-lattice:CreateServiceNetworkResourceAssociation&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;vpc-lattice:GetServiceNetworkResourceAssociation&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;vpc-lattice:ListServiceNetworkResourceAssociations&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;vpc-lattice:AssociateViaAWSService&lt;/code&gt;, plus the bedrock-agentcore target actions. Check your service control policies (SCPs) if your organization doesn’t use VPC Lattice today.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A firewall in a central inspection VPC attached to the transit gateway with appliance mode enabled.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="key-design-decisions"&gt;Key design decisions&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The snippets are excerpts from the lab templates and use the lab’s &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-agentcore-gateway-hub-spoke-inspection/blob/main/docs/lab-layout.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;address plan&lt;/a&gt;. &lt;code&gt;RGW_NET&lt;/code&gt; is the resource gateway subnets (10.0.1.0/24–10.0.3.0/24), &lt;code&gt;TOOL_NET&lt;/code&gt; is the tool VPC (10.12.0.0/16), and &lt;code&gt;ALB_NET&lt;/code&gt; is the ALB subnets in it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="decision-1-put-the-resource-gateway-in-a-dedicated-vpc-in-the-network-account"&gt;Decision 1: Put the resource gateway in a dedicated VPC in the network account&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create a dedicated VPC in the network account for the resource gateway, with subnets you don’t share and no central endpoints or backends. Route those subnets to the transit gateway. Keep one resource gateway per trust zone.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;ResourceGateway:
  Type: AWS::VpcLattice::ResourceGateway
  Properties:
    Name: hub-resource-gateway
    VpcIdentifier: !Ref ResourceGatewayVpc
    SubnetIds: [!Ref RgwSubnetA, !Ref RgwSubnetB, !Ref RgwSubnetC]
    SecurityGroupIds: [!Ref RgwSg]          # egress 443 to application ranges only
    IpAddressType: IPV4

RgwRouteToTgw:
  Type: AWS::EC2::Route
  Properties:
    RouteTableId: !Ref RgwRouteTable
    DestinationCidrBlock: 10.0.0.0/8        # internal ranges go to the transit gateway
    TransitGatewayId: !Ref Tgw&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Used only with AgentCore, the resource gateway &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/vpc-egress-private-endpoints.html#lattice-vpc-egress-compare-modes" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;consumes one IP address per subnet&lt;/a&gt;, so small subnets are enough. Two settings can’t be changed after creation, &lt;code&gt;Ipv4AddressesPerEni&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;ResourceConfigDnsResolution&lt;/code&gt;, so choose them up front. Use Availability Zone IDs that VPC Lattice and your AgentCore features support. The &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-agentcore-gateway-hub-spoke-inspection/blob/main/docs/lab-layout.md#availability-zones" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;lab layout&lt;/a&gt; lists them for &lt;code&gt;us-east-1&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="decision-2-create-one-resource-configuration-per-load-balancer-and-share-it-through-aws-ram"&gt;Decision 2: Create one resource configuration per load balancer, and share it through AWS RAM&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Point the resource configuration at the internal ALB DNS name and share it with the AgentCore account. Don’t start the name with &lt;code&gt;rcfg-&lt;/code&gt;, which VPC Lattice reserves.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;ToolsResourceConfig:
  Type: AWS::VpcLattice::ResourceConfiguration
  Properties:
    Name: tools-account-a
    ResourceConfigurationType: SINGLE
    ResourceGatewayId: !Ref ResourceGateway
    ProtocolType: TCP
    PortRanges: ["443"]
    ResourceConfigurationDefinition:
      DnsResource:
        DomainName: internal-tools-alb-1234567890.us-east-1.elb.amazonaws.com
        IpAddressType: IPV4

ShareToAgentCore:
  Type: AWS::RAM::ResourceShare
  Properties:
    Name: agentcore-resource-configs
    AllowExternalPrincipals: false
    Principals: ["&amp;lt;agentcore-account-id&amp;gt;"]
    ResourceArns: [!GetAtt ToolsResourceConfig.Arn]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;VPC Lattice delivers the connection to the resource configuration’s domain. AgentCore sends the target URL’s host name (for example &lt;code&gt;tools.example.internal&lt;/code&gt;) as the SNI and checks the ALB certificate against it. The certificate’s subject alternative name must match that host name. The host name doesn’t need to resolve in DNS.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For private API Gateway REST APIs, point one resource configuration at the network account’s &lt;code&gt;execute-api&lt;/code&gt; interface endpoint. Each API’s target uses its own URL, and each API’s resource policy must allow that endpoint through &lt;code&gt;aws:SourceVpce&lt;/code&gt;. In our tests, a second API in another account worked through the same resource configuration with no network change.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="decision-3-pass-only-the-flows-you-need-through-the-firewall"&gt;Decision 3: Pass only the flows you need through the firewall&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Your firewall needs two stateful allows: from the resource gateway subnets to the tool VPC on port 443, and from the ALB subnets to the identity provider’s host name on port 443. Session-based firewalls pass replies of an allowed flow. In the lab we used AWS Network Firewall with strict rule order and the default actions &lt;em&gt;Drop established &lt;/em&gt;and &lt;em&gt;Alert established&lt;/em&gt;. With these defaults, the firewall drops client-to-server packets that no rule passes, and lets replies through unless a rule drops them. The &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-agentcore-gateway-hub-spoke-inspection/blob/main/docs/considerations.md#aws-network-firewall-rule-order" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;rule-order notes&lt;/a&gt; explain the semantics.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;# Policy: RuleOrder STRICT_ORDER, StatefulDefaultActions [aws:drop_established, aws:alert_established]
pass tcp $RGW_NET any -&amp;gt; $TOOL_NET 443 (msg:"resource gateway to tool VPC"; flow:to_server; sid:100001; rev:4;)
pass tls $ALB_NET any -&amp;gt; any 443 (msg:"ALB to identity provider JWKS"; tls.sni; content:"cognito-idp.us-east-1.amazonaws.com"; nocase; startswith; endswith; flow:to_server; sid:100010; rev:2;)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The first rule allows only the resource gateway subnets to reach the tool VPC on port 443. The second allows the ALB to fetch its identity provider’s signing keys (JSON Web Key Set, or JWKS), only from the ALB subnets and only to the identity provider’s host name. The tool VPC needs a default route to the transit gateway for this fetch to reach central egress. If your identity provider isn’t Amazon Cognito, replace the host name. Without this rule, in our tests, the ALB returned HTTP 500 and the agent received &lt;code&gt;MCP invocation failed&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To prove replies cross the firewall, we added a drop rule for the reply direction preceding the pass rule:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;drop tcp $TOOL_NET 443 -&amp;gt; $RGW_NET any (msg:"PROOF return path blocked"; flow:to_client; sid:100098; rev:2;)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tool calls then hung until timeout, and the firewall logged &lt;code&gt;to_client&lt;/code&gt; drops for sid 100098. Removing the rule restored the calls. Run the same test on your own firewall.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="decision-4-validate-the-tool-accounts-token-on-the-internal-alb-or-private-api"&gt;Decision 4: Validate the tool account’s token on the internal ALB or private API&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The tool account keeps its own identity provider (Amazon Cognito in the lab) and creates an app client for the Gateway. Validate that client’s token on every path to the backend.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Internal ALB –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Make the listener’s default action a fixed response, and put a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/elasticloadbalancing/latest/application/listener-verify-jwt.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;jwt-validation&lt;/a&gt; action on every rule that forwards. Pin &lt;code&gt;client_id&lt;/code&gt; to the Gateway’s app client so that other clients in the same user pool are rejected.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;McpRule:
  Type: AWS::ElasticLoadBalancingV2::ListenerRule
  Properties:
    ListenerArn: !Ref Listener                # default action: fixed-response 404
    Priority: 10
    Conditions: [{Field: path-pattern, PathPatternConfig: {Values: [/mcp]}}]
    Actions:
      - Type: jwt-validation
        Order: 1
        JwtValidationConfig:
          Issuer: !Sub https://cognito-idp.${AWS::Region}.amazonaws.com/${ToolPool}
          JwksEndpoint: !Sub https://cognito-idp.${AWS::Region}.amazonaws.com/${ToolPool}/.well-known/jwks.json
          AdditionalClaims:
            - {Format: space-separated-values, Name: scope, Values: [tools/invoke]}
            - {Format: single-string, Name: client_id, Values: [!Ref GatewayAppClient]}
      - Type: forward
        Order: 2
        TargetGroupArn: !Ref ToolsTargetGroup&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In our tests, a target with no token, one with another identity provider’s token, and one with a different app client’s token were all rejected with HTTP 401 before the backend ran. The agent saw only &lt;code&gt;An internal error occurred&lt;/code&gt;, so use the ALB access logs to diagnose token problems.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Private REST API –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;An API key doesn’t authenticate the caller, so add an authorizer to every method, such as a Cognito user pool authorizer with a required scope (as in the lab). Also attach an endpoint policy to the &lt;code&gt;execute-api&lt;/code&gt; endpoint that limits it to APIs owned by approved accounts. It controls which APIs the endpoint reaches, not who calls it. The authorizer controls the caller.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{"Statement": [{"Effect": "Allow", "Principal": "*", "Action": "execute-api:Invoke",
  "Resource": ["arn:aws:execute-api:us-east-1:&amp;lt;tool-account-id&amp;gt;:*"]}]}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The ALB or endpoint security group allows only the resource gateway subnets, so the resource gateway is the only network path in. If your firewall applies source NAT, the security group must allow the firewall’s addresses instead, and it then admits anything the firewall forwards. The firewall rule and the token validation become the controls.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="decision-5-create-gateway-targets-that-reference-the-shared-resource-configuration"&gt;Decision 5: Create Gateway targets that reference the shared resource configuration&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create the Gateway with a JWT authorizer and, optionally, an AWS WAF web access control list (web ACL), which inspects the tool-call arguments for every target. AWS WAF inspects only the first 16 KB of the body by default (adjustable to 64 KB), so decide how each rule handles larger bodies. Add &lt;code&gt;AWSManagedRulesSQLiRuleSet&lt;/code&gt; to the common rule set. See the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-agentcore-gateway-hub-spoke-inspection/blob/main/docs/considerations.md#aws-waf-rule-groups-for-tool-calls" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS WAF notes&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Gateway targets with a private endpoint &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-vpc-egress.html#gateway-vpc-access-limitations" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;can’t use NO_AUTH&lt;/a&gt; for inbound authorization unless you configure an interceptor function.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each target needs an OAuth credential provider that holds the tool account’s app client ID and secret and the identity provider’s discovery URL:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;# provider.json holds the client secret, so it stays out of the command line and shell history:
# {"name": "tools-account-a", "credentialProviderVendor": "CustomOauth2",
#  "oauth2ProviderConfigInput": {"customOauth2ProviderConfig": {
#    "clientId": "&amp;lt;tool-app-client-id&amp;gt;", "clientSecret": "&amp;lt;tool-app-client-secret&amp;gt;",
#    "oauthDiscovery": {"discoveryUrl": "https://cognito-idp.&amp;lt;region&amp;gt;.amazonaws.com/&amp;lt;pool-id&amp;gt;/.well-known/openid-configuration"}}}}
chmod 600 provider.json
aws bedrock-agentcore-control create-oauth2-credential-provider --cli-input-json file://provider.json
rm provider.json&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If the ALB uses a certificate from a private certificate authority (CA), also store the CA certificate as a PEM string in an AWS Secrets Manager secret. The Gateway execution role (the AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) role you give the Gateway when you create it) needs permission to read that secret and the credential provider.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import boto3

agentcore = boto3.client("bedrock-agentcore-control", region_name="us-east-1")

agentcore.create_gateway_target(
    gatewayIdentifier="&amp;lt;gateway-id&amp;gt;",
    name="classifier",
    targetConfiguration={"mcp": {"mcpServer": {"endpoint": "https://tools.example.internal/mcp"}}},
    credentialProviderConfigurations=[{
        "credentialProviderType": "OAUTH",
        "credentialProvider": {"oauthCredentialProvider": {
            "providerArn": "&amp;lt;oauth-provider-arn&amp;gt;",
            "scopes": ["tools/invoke"],
            "grantType": "CLIENT_CREDENTIALS"}}}],
    # Shared from the network account through AWS RAM
    privateEndpoint={"selfManagedLatticeResource": {
        "resourceConfigurationIdentifier": "&amp;lt;shared-resource-configuration-arn&amp;gt;"}},
    # Trust the private CA that issued the ALB certificate
    certificateConfigurations=[{"secretsManager": {"secretArn": "&amp;lt;ca-secret-arn&amp;gt;"}}],
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An OpenAPI target uses the same blocks with &lt;code&gt;openApiSchema&lt;/code&gt; in place of &lt;code&gt;mcpServer&lt;/code&gt;. To rotate the CA, update the secret and call &lt;code&gt;UpdateGatewayTarget&lt;/code&gt;. Open connections can keep the old trust material for up to 15 minutes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Keep the Gateway the only consumer of the shared resource configuration. The AWS managed permission for AWS RAM also grants &lt;code&gt;ec2:CreateVpcEndpoint&lt;/code&gt;, so the AgentCore account could reach the backend through a resource VPC endpoint or service network of its own. A customer managed permission can’t fix this: AWS RAM rejects &lt;code&gt;vpc-lattice:AssociateViaAWSService&lt;/code&gt; in one, and without it Gateway targets fail. Attach an SCP to the AgentCore account instead:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{"Version": "2012-10-17", "Statement": [
  {"Sid": "NoOwnEndpointsToSharedResourceConfigurations", "Effect": "Deny",
   "Action": "ec2:CreateVpcEndpoint", "Resource": "arn:aws:vpc-lattice:*:*:resourceconfiguration/*"},
  {"Sid": "ServiceNetworkAssociationsOnlyThroughAwsServices", "Effect": "Deny",
   "Action": "vpc-lattice:CreateServiceNetworkResourceAssociation", "Resource": "*",
   "Condition": {"BoolIfExists": {"aws:ViaAWSService": "false"}}}]}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With this SCP, our attempts to create a resource VPC endpoint and a service network association of its own were denied, and a new Gateway target still reached &lt;code&gt;READY&lt;/code&gt;. SCPs don’t apply to the management account, so don’t run AgentCore there. In the network account, alarm on unexpected associations with an Amazon EventBridge rule that matches the AWS CloudTrail event &lt;code&gt;CreateServiceNetworkResourceAssociationBySharee&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Revoke access by deleting the Gateway targets (or the service network resource association) first, then removing the resource configuration from the share. Removing it from the share alone &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/vpc-lattice/latest/ug/sharing.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;doesn’t remove existing associations&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="decision-6-reach-the-gateway-privately-and-close-the-public-endpoint"&gt;Decision 6: Reach the Gateway privately, and close the public endpoint&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create the Gateway interface endpoint in the shared agent VPC with private DNS enabled, and attach an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/vpc-interface-endpoints.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;endpoint policy&lt;/a&gt; that lists the approved Gateways. The principal must be *, because the Gateway authenticates callers with bearer tokens. The &lt;code&gt;Resource&lt;/code&gt; list is the control.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{"Version": "2012-10-17", "Statement": [{"Effect": "Allow", "Principal": "*", "Action": "bedrock-agentcore:InvokeGateway",
  "Resource": ["arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:&amp;lt;agentcore-account-id&amp;gt;:gateway/&amp;lt;gateway-id&amp;gt;"]}]}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In our tests, a second Gateway called through the same endpoint returned JSON-RPC error -32002 &lt;code&gt;Insufficient permissions&lt;/code&gt;, but still answered on its public endpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The interface endpoint doesn’t close the Gateway’s public endpoint. Attach a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/resource-based-policies.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;resource policy&lt;/a&gt; that allows &lt;code&gt;bedrock-agentcore:InvokeGateway&lt;/code&gt; only when &lt;code&gt;aws:SourceVpce&lt;/code&gt; is your endpoint and denies it otherwise (the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-agentcore-gateway-hub-spoke-inspection/blob/main/lab-code/gateway-resource-policy.example.json" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;full policy&lt;/a&gt; is in the repository). In our tests, internet calls returned -32002 about 30 seconds after we applied it, while endpoint calls kept working. AWS notes policy changes &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/secure-ingress-connectivity-to-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-gateway-using-interface-vpc-endpoints/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;can take up to 15 minutes&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An agent with no internet route still needs its inbound JWT. Give it a path to its identity provider’s token endpoint, such as a route to the transit gateway and a firewall rule scoped like the JWKS rule in Decision 3. Alternatively, use a private identity provider.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="validate-the-pattern-in-your-environment"&gt;Validate the pattern in your environment&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you rely on the pattern, run these checks in a non-production copy of your network account, with your own firewall. The &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-agentcore-gateway-hub-spoke-inspection/blob/main/docs/validation-checks.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;full list of 14 checks&lt;/a&gt;, with how to run each and the results we saw, is in the repository, along with a &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-agentcore-gateway-hub-spoke-inspection/blob/main/docs/logging.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;log source for every hop&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Requests come from the resource gateway –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;The ALB access log shows a source IP in the resource gateway range (or your firewall’s range, if it applies source NAT).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Requests cross the firewall –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Firewall flow logs show TLS flows from resource gateway addresses to the tool account on port 443.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Replies cross the firewall –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;With a reply-direction drop rule in place (Decision 3), calls fail and the firewall logs &lt;code&gt;to_client&lt;/code&gt; drops.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The ALB and private API are token boundaries –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Targets with no token, or another client’s token, get HTTP 401 and the backend isn’t invoked.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The public Gateway endpoint is closed –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;With the resource policy attached, internet calls get -32002 and endpoint calls succeed.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If a check fails or the agent gets a generic error, the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-agentcore-gateway-hub-spoke-inspection/blob/main/docs/troubleshooting.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;troubleshooting guide&lt;/a&gt; maps each symptom to the log that shows the cause.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="considerations"&gt;Considerations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cost:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Each VPC Lattice service network resource association carries an hourly charge plus data processing. See &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/vpc/lattice/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;VPC Lattice pricing&lt;/a&gt;. Plan for one association per target. To see how many associations your targets create, compare the &lt;code&gt;resourceAssociationArn&lt;/code&gt; that &lt;code&gt;GetGatewayTarget&lt;/code&gt; returns for each target, and prefer one target per API or MCP server over many small targets.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Traffic to public endpoints:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Three calls leave the private path: the ALB’s JWKS fetch, the agent’s inbound token request, and AgentCore’s outbound OAuth token requests to each identity provider.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Account layout and quotas:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Gateway tool-call quotas apply per account as well as per gateway. Use one AgentCore account per environment, and split gateways by domain or agent group well before the limits. VPC Lattice closes idle connections, so use keepalives for long-lived MCP streams. The repository lists the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-agentcore-gateway-hub-spoke-inspection/blob/main/docs/quotas.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;other quotas&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scope of our tests:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;We used AWS Network Firewall, no source NAT, and &lt;code&gt;us-east-1&lt;/code&gt; only. Source NAT, multiple Regions, private identity providers, and OpenAPI limitations are covered in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-agentcore-gateway-hub-spoke-inspection/blob/main/docs/considerations.md#other-considerations" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;other considerations&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When your network team owns every subnet and every flow between VPCs must pass a central firewall, place the VPC Lattice resource gateway in the network account and route its subnets through the transit gateway. The network team sets up the entry point once and adds one resource configuration per backend load balancer. Application teams keep owning only their load balancers, security groups, identity providers, and Gateway targets, and AWS WAF moves from per-tool public load balancers to the Gateway. Run the validation checks in your own environment before you rely on the pattern.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

See how Stretto, the AWS Generative AI Innovation Center, and CI&amp;amp;T designed the voice architecture, validated it for sensitive conversations, and moved it to production.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;This post is co-written with Danya Gordin from Stretto and Marcelo Wzorek from CI&amp;amp;T.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before &lt;a href="https://www.stretto.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Stretto&lt;/a&gt; built an AI voice agent, consumers considering bankruptcy on its Bankruptcy.com site filled out a web form with a few fields: debt amount, attorney status, and contact information. These form-generated leads carried thin margins.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gap between a web form lead and a fully qualified lead is significant. Bankruptcy attorneys place more value on a lead that includes detailed financial data: debts, assets, vehicles, real estate, employment history, and prior filings. The web form could have been extended to capture that detail, but the longer it got, the more visitors abandoned it partway through. A phone interview would have drawn the information out naturally, because people are more willing to share financially sensitive details in conversation, but staffing and training a human intake team to run those interviews was cost-prohibitive.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Stretto saw an opportunity: replace the web form with an AI voice agent that conducts the intake interview by phone. The agent would guide callers through the full bankruptcy questionnaire conversationally and extract structured data in real time. It would then sort leads against attorney-defined rules to support attorney decision-making.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The challenge was finding a voice AI solution that could handle sensitive financial conversations naturally, with low latency, clean interruption handling, and emotional awareness, at a cost that made the unit economics work.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through how Stretto worked with the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/generative-ai/innovation-center/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Generative AI Innovation Center&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://ciandt.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CI&amp;amp;T&lt;/a&gt; (an AWS Partner) to build Tara, an AI voice agent powered by &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/nova/latest/nova2-userguide/using-conversational-speech.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Nova 2 Sonic&lt;/a&gt;, a speech-to-speech model in Amazon Bedrock. Tara conducts multi-phase bankruptcy intake interviews and sorts leads against attorney-defined rules. We cover the architecture, the phased build from validation through production deployment, and how the team tested voice quality for sensitive conversations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tara is an AI voice agent that conducts bankruptcy intake interviews over the phone. When a caller dials the Bankruptcy.com number, Tara picks up, introduces herself, and guides the caller through a structured conversation. She collects personal information, financial details, asset inventories, and employment history while adapting her tone to the caller’s emotional state. At the end of the call, Stretto’s system applies attorney-defined rules to sort the lead as qualified, disqualified, or needs-more-information for attorney review.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The solution is built on Amazon Nova 2 Sonic, a speech-to-speech foundation model (FM)available through Amazon Bedrock. It processes speech input and generates speech output natively in a single model rather than chaining separate speech-to-text, language model, and text-to-speech components. This architecture is designed to reduce latency and produce more natural-sounding conversations, both critical for the sensitive nature of bankruptcy intake calls. Building on Amazon Bedrock gave the team both Amazon Nova 2 Sonic and Anthropic’s Claude through one managed service, with no model infrastructure to run. It also brought AWS security and governance controls to a workload that handles sensitive financial data. Access to both models is governed by AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM). Traffic to Amazon Bedrock stays on the AWS network through virtual private cloud (VPC) endpoints (AWS PrivateLink). Amazon Bedrock doesn’t use prompts or model outputs to train models (for details, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/data-protection.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Data protection in Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;). Because the models run in the same AWS environment as the voice bridge, the database, and Amazon CloudWatch monitoring, the team could secure and operate the whole solution with one set of tools. Amazon Nova 2 Sonic and Amazon Bedrock are not available in all AWS Regions. For current Region availability, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock supported Regions&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The team chose Amazon Nova 2 Sonic over the previous voice solution for three reasons:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cost:&lt;/strong&gt; Stretto reports a 95 percent reduction in per-minute voice cost compared to its previous solution, based on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock on-demand pricing&lt;/a&gt; in us-east-1 as of April 2026.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Latency:&lt;/strong&gt; In Stretto’s testing, the previous solution exhibited time-to-first-audio latency above 500 milliseconds, creating awkward pauses. Amazon Nova 2 Sonic is designed for low latency to support natural conversational flow.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Interruption handling:&lt;/strong&gt; Callers in stressful situations need to interject, correct themselves, and ask clarifying questions. The previous solution handled barge-in poorly, often breaking the conversation flow. Amazon Nova 2 Sonic is designed to handle interruptions without breaking the session.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following figure shows the end-to-end architecture.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21890-1.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-21890-1.jpg" alt="Tara’s end-to-end call flow from Twilio telephony through the Amazon ECS voice bridge and Amazon Nova 2 Sonic to data persistence in Amazon Aurora" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Tara architecture showing the end-to-end call flow from telephony to data persistence&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A caller reaches Tara through Twilio, which opens a WebSocket connection into AWS. Traffic passes through AWS WAF and an Application Load Balancer to the voice bridge on Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS) on AWS Fargate, which streams audio with Amazon Nova 2 Sonic (accessed through Amazon Bedrock). After the call, Amazon Simple Queue Service (Amazon SQS) with a dead-letter queue hands the transcript to an asynchronous processor. The processor uses &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/claude/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Anthropic’s Claude 3 Haiku&lt;/a&gt; on Amazon Bedrock to extract structured fields, which persist in Amazon Aurora Serverless v2. Qualified leads route to attorneys by jurisdiction. Supporting services include NAT gateways, VPC endpoints (AWS PrivateLink), Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR), AWS Secrets Manager, and Amazon CloudWatch.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Stretto used Twilio for telephony because Tara’s intake is fully automated, with no human agents taking calls, and because Stretto wanted a direct integration with Amazon Nova 2 Sonic that kept its existing AWS backend in place. Twilio Media Streams delivers the caller’s raw audio over a WebSocket, which is the input the voice bridge needs, and CI&amp;amp;T had already integrated it with Amazon Nova 2 Sonic. For contact center workloads that route calls among human agents, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/connect/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Connect&lt;/a&gt; also supports Amazon Nova 2 Sonic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture uses the following AWS services:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/nova/latest/nova2-userguide/using-conversational-speech.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Nova 2 Sonic&lt;/a&gt; (through Amazon Bedrock): The speech-to-speech foundation model that powers Tara’s voice conversations. It handles both speech recognition and response generation in a single pass, supporting tool calling for structured data collection during the interview.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ecs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS)&lt;/a&gt; on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/fargate/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Fargate&lt;/a&gt;: A serverless compute engine that runs the voice bridge containers connecting the telephony layer to Amazon Nova 2 Sonic. The voice bridge tasks run continuously across three Availability Zones for high availability.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/rds/aurora/serverless/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Aurora Serverless v2&lt;/a&gt;: A PostgreSQL-compatible serverless relational database deployed in Multi-AZ configuration. Stores caller profiles, intake responses, qualification results, and session metadata. The serverless scaling of Aurora matches the variable call volume patterns.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Bedrock (Anthropic’s Claude 3 Haiku): Handles structured data extraction from conversation transcripts. After Amazon Nova 2 Sonic generates a transcript, Claude 3 Haiku extracts typed fields (names, dollar amounts, addresses, vehicle details) into the relational schema.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sqs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Queue Service (Amazon SQS)&lt;/a&gt; with dead-letter queues: Manages asynchronous webhook processing for post-call events, preserving failed messages for retry.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/waf/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS WAF&lt;/a&gt;, VPC Endpoints, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/elasticloadbalancing/application-load-balancer/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Application Load Balancer&lt;/a&gt;: Security and networking layers that help protect API endpoints, route traffic across Availability Zones, and provide private connectivity to AWS services.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="building-and-validating-the-voice-agent"&gt;Building and validating the voice agent&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The team took a phased approach: validate voice quality first, build the production integration, then expand the questionnaire scope after proving the system worked with real callers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="validating-voice-quality-for-sensitive-conversations"&gt;Validating voice quality for sensitive conversations&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Bankruptcy conversations are emotionally charged. Callers are often stressed, hesitant, or embarrassed. The voice agent needs to sound empathetic and natural, not robotic or transactional. Before writing any integration code, the team spent a dedicated week testing the voice quality of Amazon Nova 2 Sonic against several caller scenarios.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The validation covered:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Voice quality across caller emotional states (calm, stressed, hesitant, frustrated).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Time-to-first-audio latency benchmarks against the previous solution’s over 500 ms baseline.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Barge-in and interruption handling: can a caller interject mid-sentence without breaking the conversation?&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Emotional tone adaptation: does the agent adjust its pace and tone when a caller sounds distressed?&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This validation step produced a comparison report documenting the performance of Amazon Nova 2 Sonic against the previous solution across each dimension. The report became the go/no-go gate for proceeding to integration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="deploying-to-production-with-real-callers"&gt;Deploying to production with real callers&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With voice quality validated, the team built the end-to-end integration over three weeks. The call flow works as follows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A caller dials the Bankruptcy.com number. Twilio’s telephony layer receives the call and opens a WebSocket connection to the voice bridge running on Amazon ECS on AWS Fargate.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The voice bridge streams audio bidirectionally between the caller and Amazon Nova 2 Sonic. Tara introduces herself, collects consent, and begins the intake interview.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;After the call, the full transcript feeds into an extraction pipeline powered by Anthropic’s Claude 3 Haiku on Amazon Bedrock. The pipeline extracts the fields from the conversation and stores them in Amazon Aurora.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The full attorney-designed intake can run 15–20 minutes, which is longer than the maximum duration of a single Amazon Nova 2 Sonic streaming session (for the current value, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/nova/latest/nova2-userguide/sonic-getting-started.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Getting started with speech-to-speech&lt;/a&gt;). The team treated that session duration as a design parameter and implemented the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/nova/latest/userguide/speech-errors.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;session continuation pattern&lt;/a&gt;, an approach that carries conversation context across session boundaries. The voice bridge tracks how long each bidirectional stream with Amazon Nova 2 Sonic has been open. Shortly before the limit, it opens a new stream with the same system prompt and tool configuration and sends the conversation so far as text history. When Tara finishes her current response, the bridge switches the caller’s audio to the new stream and closes the old one, so the conversation continues smoothly for the caller.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The initial deployment covered the existing 13-field intake form, collecting the same data points the web form captured but through natural conversation. This gave the team a production baseline to measure against before expanding scope.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="expanding-the-intake-and-lead-qualification"&gt;Expanding the intake and lead qualification&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the production pilot running, the team expanded the questionnaire to the full attorney-designed intake over four weeks. This was a significant increase in scope:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Over 18 intake categories with conditional branching, where the questions Tara asks depend on the caller’s previous answers.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Complex relational data:&lt;/strong&gt; Vehicles (year, make, model, financing status, monthly payment per vehicle), real estate properties, individual debt line items, multiple employment and income sources, and prior bankruptcy filings.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Rules-based lead sorting:&lt;/strong&gt; Stretto applies attorney-defined rules to each lead, and attorneys make the final decision. Because a voice agent collects richer, conditional data than a static web form, it gives Stretto more flexibility to apply those rules and qualify leads accurately.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Lead routing:&lt;/strong&gt; Qualified leads are routed to bankruptcy practitioners, while callers who do not meet the qualification criteria are directed to alternative resources.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Amazon Aurora schema expanded to a full relational data model supporting one-to-many relationships. A single caller record might include four vehicles, two properties, and dozens of individual debt line items.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="measuring-results-and-optimizing"&gt;Measuring results and optimizing&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The final phase focused on collecting production data and tuning the system based on real caller behavior. The team tracked key performance indicators including call volume, average duration, intake completion rate, profile completeness, conversion rate, and drop-off points.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Insights from production data drove several optimizations: reordering questions to reduce early drop-offs, adjusting conditional branching to collect high-value fields earlier in the conversation, and modifying qualification thresholds based on attorney feedback. The following table shows two key production metrics.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Average call duration (completed calls)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;~8 min&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Data extraction accuracy&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;~96%&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="testing-for-quality-and-compliance"&gt;Testing for quality and compliance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deploying a voice agent that handles sensitive financial data requires more than functional testing. The AWS Generative AI Innovation Center (GenAIIC) advisory team guided the development of a comprehensive testing framework spanning four categories:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Happy path testing:&lt;/strong&gt; Complete end-to-end intake interviews with cooperative callers across the full questionnaire. These calls confirm that the over 18 categories collect accurate structured data and that the qualification rules produce correct results.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Adversarial testing:&lt;/strong&gt; Callers who provide contradictory information, change answers mid-conversation, refuse to answer certain questions, or attempt to manipulate the qualification outcome. The agent needs to handle the ambiguity and keep the conversation coherent.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Compliance testing:&lt;/strong&gt; Consent collection, disclosure delivery, and data handling workflows. The team verified that the agent delivers disclosures accurately and collects consent before proceeding, because bankruptcy intake involves sensitive financial information.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Barge-in scenario testing:&lt;/strong&gt; Callers interrupting at various points in the conversation, including during disclosures, mid-question, and during the agent’s responses. Tara should recover without losing context or asking the caller to repeat information.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="responsible-ai-controls-in-production"&gt;Responsible AI controls in production&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The team treated responsible AI as a production control, not only a testing step. Amazon Nova 2 Sonic includes built-in safety protections, described in the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/ai/responsible-ai/nova-2-sonic/overview.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS AI Service Card for Amazon Nova 2 Sonic&lt;/a&gt;. On top of those, the solution keeps consequential decisions out of the model: lead sorting runs on explicit attorney-defined rules after the call, and attorneys make the final decision. Consent and disclosures are delivered and logged before intake begins, and the adversarial test suite checks that attempts to manipulate the conversation don’t change lead outcomes. Beyond model inference, the solution uses the Amazon Bedrock bidirectional streaming API for real-time audio, tool use for structured data capture during the interview, and private connectivity to Amazon Bedrock through VPC endpoints.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The GenAIIC advisory team also provided guidance on prompt engineering best practices for voice-specific applications, including techniques for managing conversational state across session continuation boundaries and structuring system prompts that maintain consistent agent personality across long interviews.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After roughly three months in production, Stretto reported the following results:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Per-lead value:&lt;/strong&gt; Fully qualified leads with complete intake data are significantly more valuable to bankruptcy attorneys than web form leads, which capture only a few basic fields.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Lead conversion:&lt;/strong&gt; Stretto’s early production results show a 20 percent relative increase in conversion rate compared with the web form, even before the solution has been rolled out broadly across additional districts.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Data quality and call length:&lt;/strong&gt; Stretto reports that Tara extracts structured fields at approximately 96 percent accuracy in its testing, and completed calls average about 8 minutes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Voice solution cost:&lt;/strong&gt; Stretto reports a 95 percent reduction in per-minute voice cost compared to its previous solution.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“I’m incredibly grateful to the AWS GenAI Innovation Center team. Their expertise helped bring our voice agent, Tara, to life, driving a 20 percent increase in our lead conversion rate.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Danya Gordin, Principal AI Architect, Stretto&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“As one of the partners in the AWS Generative AI Partner Innovation Alliance, we can confirm that the GenAIIC program is a true catalyst, bringing customers and partners together to solve real problems and deliver business impact. With Stretto, that meant taking a voice agent to production in a matter of weeks and transform the bankruptcy intake process”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Marcelo Wzorek, Executive Manager, CI&amp;amp;T&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Stretto’s journey from web form leads to AI-assisted intake interviews illustrates what becomes possible when voice AI reaches the quality bar for sensitive use cases. By combining the speech-to-speech capabilities of Amazon Nova 2 Sonic with a well-designed architecture on AWS, the team built a voice agent that is natural enough for callers discussing personal financial hardship and cost-effective enough to deliver meaningful savings at scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The phased approach, validating voice quality first, deploying a minimal intake to production, then expanding scope based on real-world data, reduced risk at each step. The team built on validated assumptions rather than projections.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you are exploring AI voice agents for your own use case, here are some resources to get started:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Learn about &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/nova/latest/nova2-userguide/using-conversational-speech.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Nova 2 Sonic&lt;/a&gt; and speech-to-speech capabilities.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Get started with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; to access foundation models including Amazon Nova 2 Sonic.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Explore &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/fargate/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Fargate&lt;/a&gt; for serverless compute for containers.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Learn about the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/generative-ai/innovation-center/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Generative AI Innovation Center&lt;/a&gt; for advisory support on your generative AI initiatives.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you are weighing voice AI for a sensitive, high-value intake process, your AWS account team and the AWS Generative AI Innovation Center can help you scope and build it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

					<description>A recap of the inaugural AWS Quick User Group DC meetup, where data leaders explored getting enterprise data ready for AI agents. It covers what's new in AWS Quick, Financial Independence Group's AI analytics journey, and hands-on app and no-code flow building.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.linkedin.com/groups/25720044/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;em&gt;AWS Quick User Group DC&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/a&gt; &lt;em&gt;inaugural meetup recap&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Many organizations are asking how to move beyond static reporting dashboards and begin using generative and agentic AI. But before an AI agent can answer questions or help teams act, it requires a trusted data foundation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22393-1-2.jpeg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22393-1-2.jpeg" alt="Attendees at the AWS Quick User Group DC inaugural meetup" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: AWS Quick User Group DC inaugural meetup, October 2026&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On Wednesday, October 7, 2026, data leaders, developers, and practitioners gathered at the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/training/skills-centers/arlington-skills-center/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Skills Center&lt;/a&gt; in Arlington, Virginia, for the AWS Quick User Group DC meetup. The session brought together founders, software engineers, and enterprise leaders, among them a team from Financial Independence Group (FIG). Attendees explored practical strategies for modernizing legacy architectures, preparing structured data for agentic workflows, and automating routine tasks with no-code tools.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;User groups like this provide a forum for peer-to-peer learning and collaborative problem-solving. The event offered attendees an inside look at how organizations are putting &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quicksuite/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Quick&lt;/a&gt; to work to drive productivity gains.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="whats-new-with-aws-quick-agents-skills-and-connectors"&gt;What’s new with AWS Quick: Agents, skills, and connectors&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For many in the room, this meetup was a first look at what agentic AI looks like in practice with AWS Quick.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22393-2-2.jpeg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22393-2-2.jpeg" alt="Arun Ganesan presenting AWS Quick updates to the group" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Arun Ganesan presenting what’s new with AWS Quick&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The meetup kicked off by exploring the latest product capabilities across AWS Quick, focusing on its evolution into a unified AI assistant for work. Rather than functioning solely as a traditional business intelligence visualization tool, AWS Quick unifies analytics, natural-language interaction, and multi-step task orchestration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Quick agents and skills&lt;/strong&gt;: &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksuite/latest/userguide/working-with-agents.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Turn natural-language questions&lt;/a&gt; into multi-step reusable actions that aid in decision making across enterprise data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Third-party connectors&lt;/strong&gt;: AWS Quick includes built-in integrations that connect tools like Salesforce, Slack, Google Drive, and Microsoft 365, breaking down data silos.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Unified workspace&lt;/strong&gt;: A cohesive environment that bridges research, automated visual generation, and business artifact creation (presentations, documents, and spreadsheets) directly where users work.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="real-world-innovation-moving-to-ai-analytics-and-agentic-systems"&gt;Real-world innovation: Moving to AI analytics and agentic systems&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The core of the day highlighted a real-world customer journey showing how one enterprise is rethinking its data architecture so AI agents can work from governed, trusted data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="financial-independence-group-innovating-at-the-pace-of-technology"&gt;Financial Independence Group: Innovating at the pace of technology&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Vanessa Gonzalez, Vice President of AI at Financial Independence Group (FIG), shared how FIG keeps pushing past what’s possible today. They’re adopting new tools and platforms as technology changes, and building toward AI-powered analytics and agentic systems on top of their data with AWS Quick.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22393-3-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22393-3-2.png" alt="Vanessa Gonzalez presenting FIG’s AI analytics journey" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Vanessa Gonzalez, Vice President of AI at FIG, presenting&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Technology is changing fast, and FIG wants to be at the front of that change. Building on its existing analytics, FIG moved to AWS Quick, a platform that brings AI into its dashboards, reporting, and analytics. AWS Quick is FIG’s “single front door” for enterprise analytics. It brings together structured data such as customer relationship management (CRM) systems, databases, and finance data. It also pulls in unstructured content such as internal knowledge bases, policy documents, and collateral, through a governed data layer. That gives FIG a base for AI-powered insights and agentic workflows built on its own data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The AI analytics loop&lt;/strong&gt;: Moving from reporting on what already happened to a continuous cycle: Ask → Analyze → Automate → Act.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Centralized discovery&lt;/strong&gt;: An analytics portal organized by business area (sales, operations, marketing, commissions), with built-in AI chat assistants so teams can talk directly to their documents and datasets.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Democratized insights&lt;/strong&gt;: Self-service exploration tools for non-technical business users, plus a learning hub that builds prompt engineering and AI skills across teams while keeping proper governance in place.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22393-4-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22393-4-2.png" alt="Diagram of FIG’s vision for AI-powered analytics and agentic workflows on AWS Quick" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: FIG’s vision for its future with AWS Quick&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="hands-on-building-apps-and-no-code-flows"&gt;Hands-on: Building apps and no-code flows&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Attendees worked through two hands-on exercises:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Build an AWS Quick app in 50 minutes&lt;/strong&gt;: Every attendee in the room successfully designed and launched a custom application in AWS Quick. This showed how fast non-technical users and developers alike can deploy purpose-built decision tools without writing code.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Quick Flows workshop&lt;/strong&gt;: A subset of six participants dove deeper into workflow automation. They built multi-step, event-triggered flows that handle repetitive data tasks, replacing manual reporting handoffs with automated, repeatable pipelines.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22393-5-2.jpeg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22393-5-2.jpeg" alt="DC user group attendees building AWS Quick apps together" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: The DC user group building apps together&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One of the key takeaways is how quickly teams can go from an idea to a working solution with AWS Quick. New ideas now take hours or even minutes to build, not weeks or months.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="looking-ahead-and-getting-involved"&gt;Looking ahead and getting involved&lt;/h2&gt;

   &lt;td style="border: none;vertical-align: top"&gt; &lt;p&gt;The event concluded with an open group discussion where attendees shared practical observations on bridging the gap between legacy infrastructure and generative AI.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;As organizations move toward agentic architectures, the consensus from the DC user group was clear: successful AI adoption isn’t only about selecting a foundation model. It starts with intentional data design, unified access, and empowering teams to build practical automations. Building fast isn’t only about tools. It also means learning from each other, which is why events like this matter.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt;

&lt;h2 id="next-steps"&gt;Next steps&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Ready to try it yourself? Start with the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quicksuite/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Quick product page&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksuite/latest/userguide/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Quick user guide&lt;/a&gt;, then connect a data source you already use and ask your first question. From there, try building an app or an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quicksuite/flows/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Quick Flows&lt;/a&gt; automation for a task your team repeats every week.&lt;/p&gt;
