			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Amazon SageMaker AI real-time endpoints now support KV cache aware routing</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/amazon-sagemaker-ai-real-time-endpoints-now-support-kv-cache-aware-routing/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Dmitry Soldatkin]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 17 Nov 2026 21:11:51 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">577fe79ced59c54b14f189c6d3d729bc1a447e62</guid>

					<description>Amazon SageMaker AI real-time endpoints now support KV cache-aware routing, a new strategy that routes each request to the instance holding the longest cached match for its prompt. Learn how it works, when to use it compared with prefix-aware routing, and how to enable, invoke, and verify it on single model and inference component endpoints.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Today, Amazon SageMaker AI introduces KV cache-aware routing (KV-aware routing), a new routing strategy for real-time endpoints. It uses the cache state that the model container reports. It sends each request to an instance that already holds a long cached match for its prompt and isn’t too busy. It’s for teams that serve large language models (LLMs) with multi-turn conversations, long shared prompts, or heavy load. You turn it on in two places: add one environment variable to your model so the container reports its cache contents, and set &lt;code&gt;RoutingStrategy&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;KV_AWARE&lt;/code&gt; in the endpoint configuration. Your clients call the endpoint with the same APIs as before.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Here’s the problem it solves: LLM applications such as chat assistants, Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG), and agents with long system prompts send many requests that start with the same text. A model keeps the work it did on a prompt in GPU memory, the KV cache, and skips that work when the same start reaches the same instance again. In &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/reduce-llm-latency-with-prefix-aware-routing-on-amazon-sagemaker-inference/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;part 1 of this series&lt;/a&gt;, we introduced prefix-aware routing for Amazon SageMaker AI, which sends requests that start the same way to the same instance. Prefix-aware routing infers where a prefix is cached from the request alone, so it can’t tell when an instance has evicted that cache.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Prefix-aware routing picks an instance from the request alone. It hashes the start of the request, up to the &lt;code&gt;PrefixLength&lt;/code&gt; you configure (bytes of the &lt;code&gt;InvokeEndpoint&lt;/code&gt; payload, or characters of the message text for the OpenAI-compatible Chat Completions API), and sends requests that start the same way to the same instance. It never sees what an instance actually has cached. This works well when every request starts with the same shared text, and less well in two cases:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The cache on an instance changes over time. An instance evicts older KV cache entries when its GPU memory fills up, but the router keeps sending matching requests there as if the cache were still warm.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A conversation starts out short. In a multi-turn chat, each turn adds to the history. While the request is still shorter than &lt;code&gt;PrefixLength&lt;/code&gt;, every new turn changes the text the router hashes, so the next turn can land on a different instance than the one that cached the earlier turns. The turns of a conversation stay on one instance only after the request grows past &lt;code&gt;PrefixLength&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we explain how KV-aware routing works, when to use it, and how to enable, invoke, and verify it on single model endpoints and inference component endpoints.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-kv-aware-routing-works"&gt;How KV-aware routing works&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;KV-aware routing has three parts: the model container reports what it has cached, SageMaker AI keeps a managed cache index of that state for the endpoint, and the endpoint uses the index to choose an instance for each request.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you turn events on with &lt;code&gt;SM_VLLM_KV_EVENTS_CONFIG&lt;/code&gt;, the container publishes a KV cache event each time it stores or evicts cache entries, and SageMaker AI uses these events to keep the index current. The index holds only pointers: one-way, truncated hashes of cached content, each mapped to the instance or inference component copy that holds it. It never stores prompts, token IDs, request payloads, or KV tensors. When a request arrives, the endpoint tokenizes the prompt and looks up the tokens in the index. The index returns the instances or inference component copies that hold part of the prompt in their cache, and how much of it each one holds. For example, if instance A holds the cached start of a conversation, the next turn of that conversation goes to A. Because the index matches on tokens rather than a fixed number of bytes, a multi-turn history can match in full as it grows. A request that shares only part of its prompt still goes to the instance with the longest match.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The endpoint then picks among these candidates by weighing how much of the prompt each one has cached against how busy it is. A candidate that is too busy is skipped, so if the instance with the longest match is busy, the request goes to the next-best one that is available. If no candidate has a useful match or all of them are busy, the request falls back to load-aware routing, as described in Routing overhead and fallbacks. You don’t configure a concurrency threshold for this, as you do with prefix-aware routing. The index also follows the fleet. When you scale in, entries for removed instances are dropped. When you scale out or an update replaces instances, the new instances start with empty caches, so latency can be higher for a short time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ML-22381-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ML-22381-1-1.png" alt="KV-aware routing: the router uses the managed cache index to send each request to the instance holding the longest cached match" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: KV-aware routing on a SageMaker AI endpoint. The router looks up the managed cache index and forwards the request to the instance that holds the longest cached match. Each instance reports KV cache events back to the index&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In Figure 1, the endpoint has three instances, A, B, and C. Each instance runs the same model in its own model container (the AWS vLLM Deep Learning Container listed in Requirements and limits) and has its own KV cache in GPU memory, so each instance holds different cached content: A holds turns 1 to 3 of conversation 1, B holds conversations 2 and 3, and C holds document 7. When turn 4 of conversation 1 arrives (step 1), the router tokenizes it and looks it up in the managed cache index (step 2). The index shows that A holds the first 6,000 of the request’s 6,200 tokens, so the router sends the request to A (step 3), and A only has to compute the remaining tokens. If A is busy, the router sends the request to the least busy instance instead (step 4). KV-aware routing needs a choice of destinations, so it is active only with at least two instances, or at least two copies of an inference component. With a single instance, every request goes to that instance and the setting has no effect.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="routing-strategies-on-sagemaker-ai"&gt;Routing strategies on SageMaker AI&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With this launch, SageMaker AI offers four routing strategies for real-time endpoints. You set the strategy per production variant in the &lt;code&gt;RoutingConfig&lt;/code&gt; of your endpoint configuration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Strategy&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;How it routes&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Recommended for&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;RANDOM (default)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Sends each request to a randomly chosen instance.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;General-purpose workloads, non-LLM models, and requests that are interchangeable.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;LEAST_OUTSTANDING_REQUESTS&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Sends each request to the instance with the fewest in-flight requests.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Workloads where processing time varies and you want to keep all instances equally busy.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;PREFIX_AWARE&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Sends requests that share the same prompt prefix, up to PrefixLength, to the same instance. Overflows to another instance at ConcurrencyThreshold.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;LLM workloads with shared text at a fixed position at the start of the request, on any serving framework that supports prefix caching.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;KV_AWARE (new)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Sends each request to an instance that holds a matching cached prefix, based on cache state reported by the container, weighing match length against load. Falls back to load-aware routing.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;LLM workloads on a supported container with multi-turn conversations, frequent cache eviction, or requests that share only part of their start.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prefix-aware-or-kv-aware"&gt;Prefix-aware or KV-aware&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Both strategies send requests to where their prefix is likely to be cached. They differ in how they know where that is, and in what they need from your container.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prefix-aware routing&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;KV-aware routing&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Source of cache location&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Inferred from the request prefix&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cache state reported by the container&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Added latency per request&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1.3–1.9 milliseconds in part 1&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A few milliseconds for short prompts, more for long ones, mostly for tokenization&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Matching&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;First PrefixLength bytes (Invoke API) or characters (OpenAI-compatible API)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Tokens, longest cached match&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Parameters&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;PrefixLength, ConcurrencyThreshold&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;None needed&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Container&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Any container with prefix caching on&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A supported container, with prefix caching and KV cache events on (see Requirements and limits)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Request payload&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Any&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;OpenAI-compatible Completions or Chat Completions format&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="performance"&gt;Performance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We measured KV-aware routing against random routing in two ways: a large-scale benchmark on production-sized instances, and a smaller, reproducible comparison you can follow in Verify that KV-aware routing is working.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="benchmark-results"&gt;Benchmark results&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In our benchmarks with Llama 3.1 70B Instruct on seven &lt;code&gt;ml.p5.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instances, we ran 30-minute tests with 20 or 100 simulated users holding multi-turn chats and document Q&amp;amp;A sessions, with prompts of up to about 64,000 tokens. Compared with random routing, and with long prompts under load, KV-aware routing reduced p50 time to first token (TTFT) by up to 97 percent and increased throughput by up to 234 percent. It raised the KV cache hit rate from 3–48 percent to 80–93 percent. Long prompts gained even at lower load. The gain was smallest with short prompts, where there is less prefill to save. Results depend on your workload. To measure them for your own traffic, use the checks in Verify that KV-aware routing is working.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="routing-overhead-and-fallbacks"&gt;Routing overhead and fallbacks&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before it routes a request, the endpoint tokenizes the prompt and looks it up in the managed cache index. The lookup takes a few milliseconds for short prompts and longer for long ones, mostly for tokenization, but it stays small next to the time a cache hit saves. For text-only requests that use standard options, the tokens it computes are passed to the model, so the prompt isn’t tokenized twice.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If the lookup is slow, finds no useful match, or all matching instances are busy, the endpoint routes the request with load-aware routing, the same behavior as the &lt;code&gt;LEAST_OUTSTANDING_REQUESTS&lt;/code&gt; strategy. The request is still served, without the cache benefit. The same happens when the endpoint can’t tokenize a request, except in the two error cases described in Invoking the endpoint. These fallbacks aren’t reported in the SageMaker AI console or metrics at launch, so use the checks in Verify that KV-aware routing is working to confirm that routing is effective for your traffic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With very short prompts there is less prefill to save, so prefix-aware routing can be all you need.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="when-to-use-kv-aware-routing"&gt;When to use KV-aware routing&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Prefix-aware routing needs no changes to your container, but it can’t see what an instance actually holds. KV-aware routing asks the container what it holds, which makes it more precise. It pays off when there is a lot of cached context to reuse and that cache is under pressure: long prompts, high load, and instances that run short of GPU memory for the KV cache. When that happens, the container evicts the least recently used parts of the cache, including parts of shared prompts and conversation history.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For example, a support assistant runs on three instances, and each conversation begins with the same 6,000-token knowledge-base excerpt. Conversation 1 starts on instance A, which caches the excerpt. Traffic rises, A runs short of memory, and the container evicts most of that excerpt. Instance B recently served another conversation over the same excerpt and still holds its first 4,000 tokens. When the next turn of conversation 1 arrives, prefix-aware routing sends it to A again, and A recomputes the excerpt. With KV-aware routing, A’s container has already reported the eviction, so the index shows that B holds the longest match, and the request goes to B, which recomputes only the remaining tokens. At low load, A wouldn’t have evicted anything, and both strategies would have sent the request to A with the cache intact.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;KV-aware routing helps most when requests reuse cached context in ways a fixed-length prefix can’t capture:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multi-turn conversations:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Each turn sends the full conversation history, so the cached prefix grows with every turn. KV-aware routing matches the whole cached history and keeps each conversation on the instance that holds it. This matters most for long conversations. After the managed cache index reflects an eviction, the router doesn’t keep sending the conversation to an instance that has already dropped its cache.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;High-throughput inference with shared context:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;RAG applications prepend retrieved documents, and assistants send long system prompts with every request. KV-aware routing fits when these prompts are long and instances are under memory pressure. With a stable shared prefix and moderate load, prefix-aware routing also fits. Put the shared content first and in the same order: if the same documents appear in a different order, the prompts no longer share a start, so they don’t match.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Workloads where prefix length is hard to tune:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;With prefix-aware routing, you choose a &lt;code&gt;PrefixLength&lt;/code&gt; that covers your shared text without scattering requests that should stay together. KV-aware routing has no prefix length to tune, because it matches on what is actually cached.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Prefix-aware routing is a good fit, with no container changes, if your requests share a stable system prompt or document at the start, your prompts are short, or load is moderate. In these cases, instances have enough GPU memory to keep the shared text cached, so both strategies find it. Prefix-aware routing is also the choice if your container isn’t supported by KV-aware routing, because it works with any container that has prefix caching turned on. If your requests are short and interchangeable, or your model isn’t an LLM, there is little cached context to reuse, so use &lt;code&gt;RANDOM&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;LEAST_OUTSTANDING_REQUESTS&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you aren’t sure which fits, start with prefix-aware routing, run your own traffic against a &lt;code&gt;KV_AWARE&lt;/code&gt; endpoint, and compare time to first token, throughput, and hit rate as described in Verify that KV-aware routing is working.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="requirements-and-limits"&gt;Requirements and limits&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you enable KV-aware routing, check that your container, endpoint, and requests meet these requirements:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Container:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;At launch, KV-aware routing supports the AWS vLLM Deep Learning Container for SageMaker AI: images with vLLM 0.33.0 or later, or the Amazon Linux build &lt;code&gt;server-sagemaker-cuda-v2.8.0&lt;/code&gt; or later. Prefix caching must be on, which is the default, and KV cache events must be turned on with the &lt;code&gt;SM_VLLM_KV_EVENTS_CONFIG&lt;/code&gt; environment variable, as shown in How to enable it. SageMaker AI doesn’t set it for you.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Endpoints and instances:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Real-time single model endpoints and inference component endpoints, including dynamic Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) adapters, on GPU instance types. You can also configure KV-aware routing with AWS CloudFormation. Routing is active only with at least two instances for a single model endpoint, or at least two copies of an inference component, which can be on the same instance. With fewer, you can still set &lt;code&gt;KV_AWARE&lt;/code&gt; and the endpoint isn’t rejected, but every request goes to the single instance or copy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Requests:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;OpenAI-compatible Completions or Chat Completions payloads, sent with &lt;code&gt;InvokeEndpoint&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;InvokeEndpointWithResponseStream&lt;/code&gt;, or the SageMaker AI OpenAI-compatible Chat Completions API. &lt;code&gt;InvokeEndpointWithBidirectionalStream&lt;/code&gt; isn’t supported. If you set the &lt;code&gt;model&lt;/code&gt; field, it must match the value of &lt;code&gt;SM_VLLM_MODEL&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;SDK versions:&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Detailed observability, which you use to track the KV cache hit rate, needs AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI) version 2.35.6 or later, or Boto3 version 1.43.31 or later.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SageMaker AI checks these requirements before your endpoint serves traffic. If a check fails, the request or deployment fails with a message that describes what is missing. For the full list of settings, validation behavior, and quotas, see the SageMaker AI documentation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-to-enable-it"&gt;How to enable it&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You turn on KV-aware routing in two places: the model and the endpoint configuration. The &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/tree/main/03-features/kv-aware-routing" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;example notebook&lt;/a&gt; runs every step end to end, for both a single model endpoint and an inference component endpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;1. Turn on KV cache events in the model –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;When you create the model, add &lt;code&gt;SM_VLLM_KV_EVENTS_CONFIG&lt;/code&gt; to the container environment. Without it, the endpoint has no cache state to route on. The ports in this setting are used only inside the instance, so you don’t open them to your network.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;"Environment": {
    "SM_VLLM_MODEL": "&amp;lt;model-id-or-/opt/ml/model&amp;gt;",
    "SM_VLLM_KV_EVENTS_CONFIG": "{\"enable_kv_cache_events\": true, \"publisher\": \"zmq\", \"endpoint\": \"tcp://*:5557\", \"topic\": \"kv-events\", \"replay_endpoint\": \"tcp://*:5658\"}"
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;2. Choose KV-aware routing in the endpoint configuration –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Set &lt;code&gt;RoutingStrategy&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;KV_AWARE&lt;/code&gt; in the &lt;code&gt;RoutingConfig&lt;/code&gt; of the production variant:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;"RoutingConfig": {
    "RoutingStrategy": "KV_AWARE"
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If your container image is built for CUDA 13, also set &lt;code&gt;InferenceAmiVersion&lt;/code&gt; in the production variant to an AMI that supports it, such as &lt;code&gt;al2023-ami-sagemaker-inference-gpu-4-1&lt;/code&gt;. Otherwise the container can fail to start. See &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/inference-gpu-drivers.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Inference GPU drivers&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Check that your account has enough endpoint quota for the instance type and count you choose, and request an increase in Service Quotas if needed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The same &lt;code&gt;RoutingConfig&lt;/code&gt; setting works for inference component endpoints, including dynamic LoRA adapters. For adapters, KV-aware routing chooses among the instances that already have the adapter loaded.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="invoking-the-endpoint"&gt;Invoking the endpoint&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Nothing changes in how you call the endpoint. Send a Chat Completions payload with &lt;code&gt;InvokeEndpoint&lt;/code&gt;, or with &lt;code&gt;InvokeEndpointWithResponseStream&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;"stream": true&lt;/code&gt; in the request body:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{"messages": [{"role": "user", "content": "What is your return policy?"}]}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can also call the endpoint with the OpenAI Python client through the SageMaker AI OpenAI-compatible Chat Completions API. The &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/blob/main/03-features/openai/sagemaker-inference-openai-api.ipynb" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Use the OpenAI-compatible API with SageMaker AI&lt;/a&gt; notebook on GitHub shows how, for both single model endpoints and inference components.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;KV-aware routing doesn’t apply the multi-tenant prefix isolation from part 1: it ignores the prefix-aware ID header and the &lt;code&gt;prompt_cache_key&lt;/code&gt; field. A request that sets a top-level &lt;code&gt;cache_salt&lt;/code&gt; field to a non-null value is rejected with a 400 error.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;KV-aware routing adds two error cases: a request whose &lt;code&gt;model&lt;/code&gt; field names a model that the container doesn’t serve fails with a &lt;code&gt;ModelError&lt;/code&gt;, and a request with a non-null &lt;code&gt;cache_salt&lt;/code&gt; is rejected with a 400 error. Errors from inference itself are returned as before, and your model container’s own logs in Amazon CloudWatch can help you find the cause. The endpoint doesn’t deliver logs about its KV-aware routing decisions to your account, so use the checks in Verify that KV-aware routing is working, such as &lt;code&gt;cached_tokens&lt;/code&gt; per request and the KV cache hit rate, to see how routing behaves.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="verify-that-kv-aware-routing-is-working"&gt;Verify that KV-aware routing is working&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;KV-aware routing doesn’t add metrics of its own, so you check its effect on your model. There are three ways to do this.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Check cache hits per request –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;The container doesn’t report prompt token details by default, so set &lt;code&gt;SM_VLLM_ENABLE_PROMPT_TOKENS_DETAILS&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;true&lt;/code&gt; in the model environment. Each response then includes &lt;code&gt;usage.prompt_tokens_details.cached_tokens&lt;/code&gt;, the number of prompt tokens that were served from the KV cache. When you send a follow-up turn, or another request that starts the same way as an earlier one, a non-zero value shows that the request reached an instance that held its prefix.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Check model metrics in Amazon CloudWatch –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Turn on detailed observability by adding &lt;code&gt;--metrics-config '{"EnableDetailedObservability": true}'&lt;/code&gt; when you create the endpoint configuration, using the SDK versions in Requirements and limits. SageMaker AI then publishes the model container’s metrics, including the KV cache hit rate and TTFT. Query them as described in &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/monitoring-detailed-observability-promql.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Detailed observability with PromQL&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ML-22381-2-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ML-22381-2-1.png" alt="A follow-up request response with most prompt tokens served from the KV cache, and the KV cache hit rate chart in the SageMaker AI Insights dashboard" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Verifying KV-aware routing. (a) A follow-up request shows most prompt tokens served from the KV cache. (b) The KV cache hit rate for the same endpoint in the SageMaker AI Insights dashboard&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Compare with the strategy you would otherwise use –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;For most LLM workloads the real choice is between &lt;code&gt;KV_AWARE&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;PREFIX_AWARE&lt;/code&gt;, so compare those two, and add a &lt;code&gt;RANDOM&lt;/code&gt; endpoint as a baseline. Use the same model, instance type, and instance count for each endpoint, and send the same prompts at the same concurrency for the same duration, so that the only difference is the routing strategy. Make sure your test reuses context the way your production traffic does, for example with multi-turn conversations or shared documents. If &lt;code&gt;KV_AWARE&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;PREFIX_AWARE&lt;/code&gt; perform about the same for your traffic, prefix-aware routing is the simpler choice.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As an example of this comparison, we compared &lt;code&gt;RANDOM&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;KV_AWARE&lt;/code&gt; on a small endpoint. We sent the same multi-turn workload to two endpoints that differ only in routing strategy. Each endpoint had two &lt;code&gt;ml.g5.xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instances serving &lt;code&gt;Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507&lt;/code&gt; on a supported container. Each run sent 128 requests: 32 simulated users, each asking 4 questions about a document chosen from 12 documents of about 7,400 tokens. Each question resends the conversation so far, so every question after the first can reuse cached context. We ran two pairs of runs, with &lt;code&gt;RANDOM&lt;/code&gt; first in pair 1 and &lt;code&gt;KV_AWARE&lt;/code&gt; first in pair 2, and used new documents for each pair so that both endpoints started with empty caches. Table 1 shows the results as pair 1 / pair 2.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metric&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;RANDOM&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;KV_AWARE&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CloudWatch ModelLatency average (ms)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;4,124 / 5,100&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1,235 / 1,314&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CloudWatch ModelLatency p50 (ms)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3,153 / 4,083&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;656 / 700&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CloudWatch ModelLatency p90 (ms)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;8,334 / 10,746&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3,213 / 3,535&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CloudWatch ModelLatency p99 (ms)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;17,779 / 16,249&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;6,876 / 7,238&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Time to first token p50, questions 2–4 (ms)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1,284 / 1,632&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;237 / 245&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Time to first token p90, questions 2–4 (ms)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;5,039 / 6,101&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;456 / 367&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Time to first token p90, all requests (ms)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;5,003 / 5,683&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1,465 / 1,759&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Share of prompt tokens served from cache&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0.761 / 0.701&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0.903 / 0.903&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Run duration (s)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;31.1 / 36.9&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;17.9 / 18.0&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CloudWatch OverheadLatency average (ms)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;13.1 / 8.1&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;27.1 / 27.5&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Table 1: RANDOM and KV_AWARE routing under the same workload. Each cell shows pair 1 / pair 2. ModelLatency and OverheadLatency are Amazon CloudWatch metrics for the endpoint. The client measured time to first token and run duration. The cache share is the sum of cached_tokens divided by the sum of prompt_tokens over a run&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In both pairs, &lt;code&gt;KV_AWARE&lt;/code&gt; had lower CloudWatch ModelLatency (average 1,235 and 1,314 ms against 4,124 and 5,100 ms) and lower time to first token for questions 2–4 (p50 237 and 245 ms against 1,284 and 1,632 ms). Its cache share was higher, at 90 percent in both pairs against 76 and 70 percent. OverheadLatency, the time SageMaker AI spends outside the model container, was 14 to 19 ms higher on average with &lt;code&gt;KV_AWARE&lt;/code&gt;. This is one model, one instance type, and one workload with long shared documents, so measure your own traffic as described earlier in this section. Figure 3 shows the model latency charts that the SageMaker AI console displays for the two endpoints.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ML-22381-3-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/17/ML-22381-3-1.png" alt="One-minute average model latency in CloudWatch for the RANDOM endpoint in blue and the KV_AWARE endpoint in orange across seven runs" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: One-minute average model latency in Amazon CloudWatch for the RANDOM endpoint (blue) and the KV_AWARE endpoint (orange), on a shared 0–7 second axis. Each endpoint received seven consecutive runs of the same workload on new documents, RANDOM first. Average model latency was 4.9–6.2 seconds for RANDOM and 1.2–1.3 seconds for KV_AWARE. The last RANDOM point is lower because that minute holds only the last 20 requests of the run&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If neither does better than &lt;code&gt;RANDOM&lt;/code&gt;, check that prefix caching and KV cache events are turned on in the container, as described in Requirements and limits.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="example-notebook"&gt;Example notebook&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The example &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/tree/main/03-features/kv-aware-routing" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;notebook&lt;/a&gt; applies the steps in this post to &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Qwen/Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507&lt;/a&gt; on two &lt;code&gt;ml.g5.xlarge&lt;/code&gt;&amp;nbsp;instances, the minimum for a single model endpoint, and on an inference component endpoint with two copies. It calls the endpoint with both APIs, shows in the container logs that follow-up requests are served from the KV cache, and doesn’t compare routing strategies. Run its cleanup cells at the end to avoid ongoing charges.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete the resources you created. For an inference component endpoint, delete the inference components first (&lt;code&gt;delete-inference-component&lt;/code&gt;), then the endpoint, endpoint configuration, and model (&lt;code&gt;delete-endpoint&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;delete-endpoint-config&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;delete-model&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="practical-guidance"&gt;Practical guidance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These three practices help you keep KV-aware routing effective after you turn it on.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Monitor the KV cache hit rate –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Keep detailed observability on after launch and watch the KV cache hit rate over time in the SageMaker AI Insights dashboard (Figure 2b), or query it with PromQL, as described in Verify that KV-aware routing is working.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Plan for cold starts –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;The first request with new context, such as a new document or the first turn of a conversation, has nothing cached on any instance. KV-aware routing speeds up the requests that follow, not the first one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Plan for cache rebuilds during scaling and updates –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;As described in How KV-aware routing works, new instances start with empty caches. To soften the latency increase, use a rolling or blue/green update for single model endpoints, or a &lt;code&gt;RollingUpdatePolicy&lt;/code&gt; for inference components.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;KV-aware routing gives SageMaker AI real-time endpoints a second way to send requests to where their prompts are cached. It uses the cache state that your model container reports, so it follows the cache when instances evict it under memory pressure. Prefix-aware routing from &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/reduce-llm-latency-with-prefix-aware-routing-on-amazon-sagemaker-inference/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;part 1 of this series&lt;/a&gt; remains the simpler choice for stable shared prompts, short contexts, and moderate load. SageMaker AI handles routing inside the endpoint for both strategies, so you don’t build or run a routing layer yourself. You turn it on with one environment variable in the model and &lt;code&gt;"RoutingStrategy": "KV_AWARE"&lt;/code&gt; in the endpoint configuration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;KV-aware routing is available today in all commercial AWS Regions that support SageMaker AI real-time inference, for endpoints that use a supported container. It is available at no additional charge. SageMaker AI runs and manages the cache index for you, and you pay only for the instances your endpoint uses. &lt;code&gt;KV_AWARE&lt;/code&gt; is a new value for the existing &lt;code&gt;RoutingStrategy&lt;/code&gt; field, so you don’t need a new configuration object, and older versions of the AWS CLI and Boto3 accept it. To get started, read the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_ProductionVariantRoutingConfig.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;documentation&lt;/a&gt;, run the example &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/tree/main/03-features/kv-aware-routing" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;notebook&lt;/a&gt; on a small endpoint, and then compare &lt;code&gt;KV_AWARE&lt;/code&gt; with &lt;code&gt;PREFIX_AWARE&lt;/code&gt; on a copy of your own traffic.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Govern and discover records with common metadata in AWS Agent Registry</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/govern-and-discover-records-with-common-metadata-in-aws-agent-registry/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Sana Iqbal]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 17 Nov 2026 20:31:04 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">93acfc16dc33f85c194155e4b06fadd97e3c1175</guid>

					<description>Custom metadata schemas in AWS Agent Registry let an administrator define typed, validated fields that every agent, MCP server, and skill must carry. This post follows an MCP record from schema definition through registration, enrichment, approval, and discovery, so teams govern and find resources with one shared set of facts.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;As organizations build more agents, Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers and skills, they need a consistent way to describe, govern, and find them. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/manage-agents-tools-and-skills-at-scale-with-aws-agent-registry/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Agent Registry&lt;/a&gt; provides a catalog for registering, approving, and discovering these resources and custom records. Each record carries a name, a description, a protocol descriptor, and an approval status. However, every organization has attributes and facts that it wants to track, and some are unique to that organization, such as its business units, resource owners, risk classifications, and development environments. Organizations want one common definition for each of these attributes, and they want every agent, MCP server, and skill in the registry to carry these facts. Until now, organizations had no way to define these facts in AWS Agent Registry and store them on each record.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Custom metadata schemas are now available in AWS Agent Registry. A custom metadata schema is a set of organization-defined key-value pairs that records in an AWS Agent Registry carry. An administrator defines the schema and applies it to the registry, and all records within the registry must then adhere to the schema. When publishers create or submit records, they supply values for the fields specified in the applicable schema, and AWS Agent Registry validates those values against the schema set by the admin. Approvers can rely on the validated values when reviewing a record, and consumers and agents can use them to discover and filter records. Administrators, publishers, security and governance teams, approvers, consumers, and agents all work from a shared set of facts to govern, discover, and understand the resources in their agentic fleet.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we follow the AnyCompany organization publishing an MCP server with custom schema definition, enriching that record, and making it available for discovery by humans or agents. We use an MCP server as the example, but the same steps apply to other record types.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-custom-metadata-works"&gt;How custom metadata works&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With custom metadata, an organization can use standardized vocabulary for describing its resources. The registry administrator defines the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/registry-custom-metadata-schema.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;custom metadata schema&lt;/a&gt;: field names, types, allowed values, and whether each field is required or optional. The registry enforces the schema on every record, so a field such as &lt;code&gt;environment&lt;/code&gt; has the same name and the same allowed values whichever team publishes the record.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The schema has common attributes that apply to every record type, and you can add dedicated fields that only apply to specific record types. Each field is Text, Enum, URL, or Boolean.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;When a record is created or updated, the registry validates the custom metadata against the schema set up by the admin.&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;If a record is created &lt;strong&gt;&lt;em&gt;without any custom metadata,&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/strong&gt; AWS Agent Registry saves it as &lt;code&gt;NON_COMPLIANT&lt;/code&gt;. A team can save work in progress this way, but it can’t submit the record for approval until it has the required fields and values.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;If a record &lt;strong&gt;&lt;em&gt;includes custom metadata&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/strong&gt; with an unknown field, a missing required field, or an invalid value, AWS Agent Registry rejects it with a &lt;code&gt;ValidationException&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;GetRegistryRecord&lt;/code&gt; returns the record’s custom metadata values and its compliance status. (&lt;code&gt;COMPLIANT&lt;/code&gt; means that the values match the administrator’s schema. It is not an approval. The approvers make that decision.)&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;Discovery APIs also allow filtering records based on custom attributes.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-custom-metadata-makes-possible"&gt;What custom metadata makes possible&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because teams can describe the AWS Agent Registry records with the same fields and values, the registry can support and scale governance and compliance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="ownership-and-operations"&gt;Ownership and operations&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Fields such as &lt;code&gt;environment&lt;/code&gt; (&lt;code&gt;prod&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;staging&lt;/code&gt;, or &lt;code&gt;dev&lt;/code&gt;), &lt;code&gt;businessUnit&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ownerTeam&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;supportContact&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;costCenter&lt;/code&gt; record where a resource runs, who is responsible for it, and who pays for it. A configuration management database (CMDB) ID links the record to your existing inventory, and a support-tier field sets expectations for anyone who depends on the resource.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="security-and-compliance"&gt;Security and compliance&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Data classification, compliance scope such as Payment Card Industry (PCI) or Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act (HIPAA), risk tier, scan status, and a scan report URL turn an approval into a check of structured evidence. Your existing scanners, CMDB sync jobs, and classification tools write these values through the API before a record is submitted. You can build your record approval process or automation using these values, as this post shows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="better-discovery"&gt;Better discovery&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Custom metadata values are indexed for search. A consumer or agent can combine a natural-language query with specific metadata filters, such as &lt;code&gt;environment=prod AND businessUnit=payments AND securityScanStatus=PASSED&lt;/code&gt;. Only approved records are returned, so a record that matches the filters but hasn’t been approved stays out of the results. &lt;em&gt;Figure 1&lt;/em&gt; shows how each role, from administrator to consumer, moves a record through schema definition, registration, enrichment, approval, and discovery.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22167-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22167-1.png" alt="Swimlane diagram showing how a record moves through AWS Agent Registry across the administrator, publisher, approver, and consumer roles" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Swimlane diagram showing how a record moves through AWS Agent Registry, by role&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="walkthrough-of-an-mcp-record-from-registration-to-discovery"&gt;Walkthrough of an MCP record from registration to discovery&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AnyCompany operates &lt;code&gt;anycompany-governed-registry&lt;/code&gt;, an AWS Agent Registry without auto-approval. Its payments team publishes an MCP record, &lt;code&gt;payments-ledger-mcp&lt;/code&gt;, a read-only server for ledger lookups. Existing security and inventory systems enrich this MCP record before an approval function decides whether consumers can discover it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The five numbered steps in &lt;em&gt;Figure 2&lt;/em&gt; match the walkthrough: define, register, enrich and submit, review, then discover.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22167-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22167-2.png" alt="Five-step flow showing custom metadata gathered from existing tools feeding both the approval decision and discovery search in AWS Agent Registry" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Custom metadata gathers publisher context and values from your existing tools, so approval and search can use the same facts&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To follow the walkthrough, you need:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;An AWS account in a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agentcore-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;supported AWS Region&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;AWS credentials available to Boto3 and AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) permissions for AWS Agent Registry.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;Python 3.11 or later and boto3 1.43.113 or later.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;The &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/07-centralize-and-govern-your-ai-infrastructure/03-registry/03-advanced/custom-metadata-in-registry" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample implementation&lt;/a&gt; is in the GitHub repo.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The snippets are shortened: &lt;code&gt;cp&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;dp&lt;/code&gt; are the &lt;code&gt;agent-registry-control&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;agent-registry&lt;/code&gt; boto3 clients. The sample repository has the complete code.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-aws-agent-registry-admin-defines-the-custom-metadata-schema-for-the-registry"&gt;Step 1: AWS Agent Registry admin defines the custom metadata schema for the registry&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AnyCompany’s admin creates a registry with a custom metadata schema that every record must follow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="a"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Every record must have&lt;/strong&gt; its &lt;code&gt;environment&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;businessUnit&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;securityScanStatus&lt;/code&gt; fields.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;Records can also carry optional fields that the publisher or automation fills in later: &lt;code&gt;ownerTeam&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;cmdbId&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;dataClassification&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;securityScanReportUrl&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;MCP servers also record their &lt;code&gt;networkEgress&lt;/code&gt;, which is optional.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;default_schema = {"type": "object", "properties": {
    "environment": {"type": "string", "enum": ["prod", "staging", "dev"]},
    "businessUnit": {"type": "string"},
    "ownerTeam": {"type": "string"},
    "cmdbId": {"type": "string"},
    "dataClassification": {"type": "string", "enum": ["public", "internal", "confidential", "restricted"]},
    "securityScanStatus": {"type": "string", "enum": ["PENDING", "PASSED", "FAILED"]},
    "securityScanReportUrl": {"type": "string", "format": "uri"},
}, "required": ["environment", "businessUnit", "securityScanStatus"]}

mcp_schema = {"type": "object", "properties": {"networkEgress": {
    "type": "string", "enum": ["none", "vpc-only", "allowlisted-internet"]}}}

cp.create_registry(
    name="anycompany-governed-registry",
    approvalConfiguration={"autoApprovalRules": []},
    customMetadataSchemaConfiguration={
        "defaultSchema": json.dumps(default_schema),
        "recordTypeSchemaOverrides": [
            {"recordType": "MCP", "schema": json.dumps(mcp_schema)}]})&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 3 shows this schema and the values &lt;code&gt;payments-ledger-mcp&lt;/code&gt; will carry once enrichment completes in Step 3. The empty &lt;code&gt;autoApprovalRules&lt;/code&gt; list means that the registry approves nothing on its own. Every submitted record stays in &lt;code&gt;Pending approval&lt;/code&gt; until someone or something approves it. You decide whether that is done by automated checks you build (Step 4 uses an AWS Lambda function), a human approver, or both.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22167-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22167-3.png" alt="Diagram of the registry’s common attributes, the fields added for MCP records, and the validated custom metadata map for payments-ledger-mcp" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Diagram showing the registry’s common attributes, the fields added for MCP records, and the validated customMetadata map for payments-ledger-mcp&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Administrators can also review the schema in the AWS Agent Registry console. Figure 4 shows the common attributes and the MCP-specific field as they appear on the registry’s details page.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22167-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22167-4.png" alt="AWS Agent Registry console showing the custom metadata schema with its common attributes and the MCP-specific field" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: The custom metadata schema in the AWS Agent Registry console&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-the-publisher-registers-the-server"&gt;Step 2: The publisher registers the server&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The payments team’s pipeline registers &lt;code&gt;payments-ledger-mcp&lt;/code&gt; with its custom metadata. Its first attempt uses &lt;code&gt;production&lt;/code&gt; instead of one of the administrator’s allowed values.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;customMetadata sent by the pipeline:

{
    "environment": "production",
    "businessUnit": "payments",
    "securityScanStatus": "PENDING"
}

ValidationException: Custom metadata validation failed for record type MCP: /environment: does not have a value in the enumeration ["prod", "staging", "dev"]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The registry rejects the record. After the pipeline sends &lt;code&gt;prod&lt;/code&gt;, the record is created as a compliant draft. This is how the schema keeps every team using the same values.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-automation-adds-additional-metadata-and-submits"&gt;Step 3: Automation adds additional metadata and submits&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Next, AnyCompany’s automation adds what its own systems know: the result from its security scanner, the asset ID from its CMDB, and the approved data classification. It then submits the record for approval.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;record = cp.get_registry_record(registryId=registry_id, recordId=record_id)
scan = run_security_scan(record)  # Replace this stub with your scanner call.
metadata = {**record["customMetadata"],
    "cmdbId": get_cmdb_id(record),
    "dataClassification": get_classification(record),
    "securityScanStatus": scan["status"],
    "securityScanReportUrl": scan["report_url"]}
cp.update_registry_record(registryId=registry_id, recordId=record_id,
    customMetadata={"optionalValue": metadata})
wait_for_record(cp, registry_id, record_id, "DRAFT")
cp.submit_registry_record_for_approval(
    registryId=registry_id, recordId=record_id)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An update &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/registry-custom-metadata-values.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;replaces the whole metadata map&lt;/a&gt;, so the automation reads the current values, adds its own, and writes them back together. In the sample, the scanner and CMDB calls are stubs that you replace with your own tools. The registry sends a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/registry-notifications-approvals.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;pending-approval event&lt;/a&gt; to Amazon EventBridge. In Step 4, a rule that you create passes the event to your approval function.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-the-approval-automation-decides-from-the-evidence"&gt;Step 4: The approval automation decides from the evidence&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When the record moves to Pending approval, AWS Agent Registry sends an event to Amazon EventBridge, and an Amazon EventBridge rule is set to invoke a custom AWS Lambda function. &lt;em&gt;Figure 5&lt;/em&gt; shows the console view of &lt;code&gt;payments-ledger-mcp&lt;/code&gt;, with the custom metadata values that the approval function reads for its check.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22167-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-22167-5.png" alt="AWS Agent Registry console showing the custom metadata values on the payments-ledger-mcp record" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: The custom metadata on the record is the evidence that the approval function will check&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The function reads the record’s custom metadata and compliance status to check that the record is &lt;code&gt;COMPLIANT&lt;/code&gt;, the scan &lt;code&gt;PASSED&lt;/code&gt;, a &lt;code&gt;cmdbId&lt;/code&gt; is present, and the data is public or internal. &lt;code&gt;payments-ledger-mcp&lt;/code&gt; passes all four, so the function approves. A record that fails a check is rejected, and the reason names the failed check. In your production systems, you can choose to route records that need a human review, such as those with confidential or restricted data, for manual review instead of being rejected.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-5-consumers-find-the-records-from-aws-agent-registry-with-custom-metadata-fields-as-filters"&gt;Step 5: Consumers find the records from AWS Agent Registry with custom metadata fields as filters&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A developer looking for a ledger tool searches the registry and filters on the same fields the administrator defined.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;dp = boto3.client("agent-registry")
matches = dp.search_discoverable_registry_records(
    registryIds=[registry_id],
    searchQuery="ledger lookup",
    filters={"$and": [
        {"customMetadata.environment": {"$eq": "prod"}},
        {"customMetadata.businessUnit": {"$eq": "payments"}},
        {"customMetadata.securityScanStatus": {"$eq": "PASSED"}},
    ]},
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The search returns &lt;code&gt;payments-ledger-mcp&lt;/code&gt; because it’s approved and matches all three filters. Agents can run the same search through the registry’s MCP endpoint, &lt;code&gt;https://agentregistry.&amp;lt;region&amp;gt;.api.aws/registry/&amp;lt;registryId&amp;gt;/mcp&lt;/code&gt;, which offers search, list, and get as MCP tools. In the GitHub sample, a Strands agent connects to the AWS Registry MCP endpoint and uses a model on Amazon Bedrock. Its system prompt lists the custom metadata fields to filter records on. The agent turns a plain-language request, such as “a production MCP server for payments ledger lookups that passed its security scan”, into a filtered search. It answers with &lt;code&gt;payments-ledger-mcp&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Developers and agents query the same fields the administrator defined, and the approval function checked, so both find only servers that meet your governance rules.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="design-the-schema-for-long-term-use"&gt;Design the schema for long-term use&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Treat the schema as your organization’s shared vocabulary:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;Agree on the vocabulary first. Settle field names and allowed values with the teams that publish and approve records before you create the schema. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/registry-custom-metadata-schema.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Schema changes are additive&lt;/a&gt;: you can add fields, add allowed values, or make an optional field required, but you can’t rename, remove, or retype a field.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;Expect existing records to lag behind schema changes made after the records were added to the registry. Existing records aren’t rewritten. A record that no longer matches the schema is reported as &lt;code&gt;NON_COMPLIANT&lt;/code&gt; and keeps its approval status. The record stays approved, but its next update must meet the new schema. Editing an approved record creates a draft that must be submitted for approval again, and the submit fails until every required field has a value. Until a curator approves the new revision, consumers keep seeing the approved one.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;Put each field at the right level. Use common attributes for questions that apply to every record type, and record-type fields for record-specific facts such as &lt;em&gt;autonomy level&lt;/em&gt; for agents.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;Pick the type that fits the field. Use Enums for decision fields that need consistent spelling, Text for external identifiers, and URL for evidence stored behind the source system’s access controls.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;Make a field required only when the publisher can assign a value at registration. For a value that your continuous integration (CI) or automation provides after the record is published, allow an interim value, for example &lt;code&gt;PENDING&lt;/code&gt;. In this post, the scan runs after registration, so &lt;code&gt;securityScanStatus&lt;/code&gt; has three values: &lt;code&gt;PENDING&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;PASSED&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;FAILED&lt;/code&gt;. The publisher writes &lt;code&gt;PENDING&lt;/code&gt;, and the enrichment automation replaces it.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;Keep sensitive details out. Leave secrets, internal endpoints, and detailed findings out of broadly readable metadata.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed how to add custom metadata in AWS Agent Registry to give every record a common set of facts, such as its environment, owning business unit, data classification, and security scan status. Using one MCP server, we showed an administrator defining the schema, a publisher and existing automation filling in the values, an approval function that you build in AWS Lambda deciding approval, and a consumer finding the approved server with metadata filters. Because the administrator owns the schema, every team uses the same fields and values, and that consistency is what makes approvals and searches in AWS Agent Registry dependable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Try it yourself:&lt;/strong&gt; Use the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/07-centralize-and-govern-your-ai-infrastructure/03-registry/03-advanced/custom-metadata-in-registry" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample at GitHub repo&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Go deeper:&lt;/strong&gt; Read the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/registry.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Agent Registry Developer Guide&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/agent-registry-control/latest/APIReference/API_CustomMetadataSchemaConfiguration.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;custom metadata API reference&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Availability:&lt;/strong&gt; See &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agentcore-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;supported AWS Regions&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear:both"&gt; &lt;/p&gt;
